        'Intelligent eyewear' has a built-in camera and mic, but there's a recording light to warn you and some stern legalese to discourage abuse
    
It’s been nearly a decade since Google ditched its ill-fated ""Glass"" product, an early attempt at making smart glasses. Now, as rivals like Meta enter the market with arguably more stylish kit, the Chocolate Factory has returned to the market and this time promises it won't invade your privacy.
As announced Thursday, Google has decided not to make wearables itself, instead turning Samsung and eyeglass makers Warby Parker and Gentle Monster. Like most of the rest of this generation of smart glasses, none of the nine styles available feature heads-up displays, just cameras and audio feedback from Gemini.
Google suggests its smart specs give users a chance to ask its Gemini chatbot questions about what they see, or have the AI speak turn-by-turn directions. Wearers of the device can also push a button or speak a command to activate the built-in 12MP camera and shoot snaps or video.
Warby Parker's version of the glasses will come in three styles, with prescription and sunglass lenses available. Prices start at $395. The same lens options are available in the six frames from Gentle Monster, which will start at $449. Both manufacturers promise nine hours of battery life, boosted by charging cases that can refresh the glasses up to seven times.
Preorders for both are available now, with shipments arriving, and sales beginning, on December 10.
Google promises privacy
The Chocolate Factory, not exactly known for being reliable with its privacy claims, is insisting that this latest generation of smart glasses won’t produce another generation of “glassholes,” folk whose focus on wearable devices became synonymous with being creepy and rude to those around them.
Google has some catchup to do to that end, given Meta’s smart glasses have already landed it in hot water over privacy concerns that have led to some calling the devices “pervert glasses.”
Unsurprisingly, at around the same time it announced its new ""intelligent eyeware,"" Google published a blog post detailing how it intends to mitigate privacy concerns.
As with similar headsets, there’s a little light that comes on whenever the camera is activated. In Google’s case, it claims that light is tamper-resistant and designed to disable recording if something as small and simple as a bit of hair blocks a view of the light. It’s not clear how the tamper proofing works from Google’s statements, but a spokesperson told us a combination of hardware and software is used to detect attempts to disable or disrupt privacy features.
“We're committed to continuously improving the effectiveness of these solutions as new attack vectors are identified,” Google told us.
The glasses don't save images or video captured by the camera into a user’s Gemini history, Google said, nor are visuals used to train the company’s AI models or target advertisements. Google told us it does save audio recordings and transcripts of conversations with Gemini that users create while wearing the glasses but again insists that those aren’t used to train its AI models.
We asked about serving ads too, but Google’s responses didn't address the topic.
Lucky for those in range of a pair of Google glasses when wearers ask Gemini to interpret images captured by the device's camera, the tech specs redact the faces of strangers before they’re transmitted. Google told us that face redaction doesn’t apply to photos or videos saved to a wearer’s camera roll.
Google’s terms of use require users not to record others without permission, or in ""sensitive places."" If users ignore that requirement, the company reserves the right to brick their devices. Another restriction means Google won't allow apps that use the glasses for real-time streaming or facial recognition into its Play app store.
Given that it remains possible to use the glasses to store recordings of people around a wearer, it’s safe to assume non real-time facial recognition is still a possibility. Creeps will doubtless use such loopholes to behave abominably. ®

67,2026-11-12,"Home sales dropped 5.5 percent in October, the lowest level since August 2010.
Save
Share
Sales of previously owned homes in the US have hit the lowest level since 2010 as mortgage rates hover above 7 percent.
The National Association of Realtors (NAR) said on Thursday that home sales dropped 5.5 percent in October to a seasonally adjusted annual rate of 3.75 million units. That was the lowest level since August 2010.
“Home buying is difficult when mortgage rates have climbed to their highest levels since 2000, aside from a single month in 2023. As a result, home sales fell to their lowest level since August 2010,” said NAR Chief Economist Lawrence Yun.
He said, “It is just a tough market for buyers and sellers.”
“Sales decreased by 8.8 percent year-over-year,” the NAR report found. “Month-over-month sales declined in the Midwest, South and West and held steady in the Northeast. Year-over-year sales declined in all four regions.”
The home sales used in the report are counted at the closing of a contract and are a look at previously occupied ones. October’s sales likely reflected contracts signed in August and September.
“Despite fewer transactions in October, the 88 million homeowners across the country are largely in a comfortable financial position due to sizable housing wealth accumulation,” said the report.
First-time buyers accounted for just 29 percent of home purchases last month, down from 31 percent in September and 32 percent in October last year, NAR said. First-time buyers have historically made up closer to 40 percent of home sales.
The US housing market has been in a slump since 2022, when mortgage rates increased from pandemic-era lows. Sales of previously occupied US homes were essentially flat last year, stuck at a 30-year low. Yun expects home sales this year will likely end up lower than in 2025.
The average 30-year fixed-rate mortgage was 7.33 percent in October, according to the Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, more commonly known as Freddie Mac, up from 6.86 percent in September and 6.25 percent one year ago.
The rate, however, averaged 7.18 percent last week, down from 7.24 percent in the previous week.
Rates on home loans are influenced by inflation, Federal Reserve policy and bond-market investors’ expectations for the economy, among other factors. They generally follow the trajectory of the 10-year Treasury yield, which lenders use as a guide to pricing home loans.
Until the past few weeks, mortgage rates had been on the rise since late February, when the US war with Iran began, pushing up the price of oil and driving inflation higher. The run-up in rates also reflected heightened bond market volatility, which led to rising yields on US Treasuries.

68,2026-11-12,"maxxing the ability to run different LLMs on the smallest devices possible



Run the biggest language model you can on the smallest hardware you own.
TV boxes, old phones and tablets, single-board computers and routers all have a CPU, some RAM and flash storage that sit idle most of the day. smolmaxxing finds the biggest model each of them can really run, then pushes it further with custom kernels, careful memory use and weights streamed from flash. Every number in this repo was measured on the device itself.
The TV box and the iPad run 8B and 16B mixture-of-experts models on about 1 GB of RAM, and the phone runs 35B and 80B ones with about 3 GB free. They all keep only part of each model in memory and read the rest from flash as it's needed.
The chart below compares the two 1 GB devices size class by size class; the phone plays in a bigger league, and its results are in docs/phone.md.

More devices (single-board computers, routers, more phones) are coming. The techniques aren't tied to one box: the 1-bit kernel needs only basic NEON, and the flash streaming works on any Linux or Android device with more model than RAM.
Got a small device? Run a model on it and submit your numbers: we'll add them to the results.

All of it is a patch on llama.cpp. The main ideas:
docs/how-it-works.md has every technique with its measured gain.
The quickest start is a ready-made model file from Hugging Face. The TV box and iPad files need the patched llama.cpp (stock llama.cpp can't load their pruned heads); the phone files load anywhere, but their speeds need the patch's expert pager.
Other people running language models on hardware that was never meant for them:
MIT. The llama.cpp patches are derivatives of llama.cpp (MIT). Model weights keep their own licenses, and the evaluation texts are Wikipedia excerpts (CC BY-SA 4.0).
Built on llama.cpp and llama2.c. Models from Qwen, unsloth (GGUFs and importance matrices), PrismML (Bonsai), inclusionAI (Ling-mini-2.0), tablinum (Moon-Beam), bartowski and mradermacher (GGUFs), Google (Gemma) and stories260K. The iPad is jailbroken with Amethyst. The banner uses Archivo (SIL OFL) and is rendered by tools/banner/render.sh. The 🤏 logo is the pinching-hand emoji from Google's Noto Emoji (Apache 2.0).

69,2026-11-12,"Strategy shares declined Thursday following MSCI’s decision to remove the company from its indexes, as Bitcoin also fell amid a broader crypto market decline.

Shares of Michael Saylor’s Bitcoin treasury company Strategy fell 6.49% on Thursday, closing at $114.41, a day after MSCI confirmed the company’s removal from its global equity indexes and amid a broader cryptocurrency market sell-off.
On Wednesday, MSCI confirmed that Strategy would be removed from its global equity indexes after the Nov. 30 market close, a move that could trigger selling by funds tracking the benchmarks. JPMorgan previously estimated that the exclusion could result in roughly $2.8 billion in selling pressure.
Source: Yahoo Finance
Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, told Cointelegraph that MSCI’s decision could help distinguish Bitcoin treasury companies from traditional operating businesses and limit indirect cryptocurrency exposure for passive investors. 
“Index inclusion meant ordinary passive investors, like someone holding a global index fund in their pension, were getting leveraged Bitcoin exposure they never chose. MSCI just fixed that,” he said. 
Kirkley added that MSCI’s decision also raises broader questions about whether Bitcoin treasury companies should be classified alongside traditional operating businesses.
In its Aug. 31 response to MSCI’s proposed rules, Strategy argued that the changes were a pretext for targeting digital asset treasury companies. It also downplayed the potential financial impact, saying MSCI-linked funds held approximately 13 million of its shares, representing just 3.1% of its basic shares outstanding.
The stock’s decline coincided with a broader cryptocurrency market sell-off. Bitcoin traded near $71,340 as of 4:08 p.m. ET on Thursday, down 4.3% over the past 24 hours, according to CoinGecko data. The total cryptocurrency market capitalization declined 3% to around $2.49 trillion.
Magazine: Bitcoin’s ‘best buying opportunity ever’ has arrived: Mark Moss

CNBC reports that Nvidia stock is down more than ten percent in pre-market trading as of 8:58 a.m. EST. The stock is now down more than 20 percent over the past month, in large part due to Huang’s China spy scandal. Since this story was first published, Nvidia announced that Jensen Huang has resigned from his position as CEO.
The slide follows a rough week for the chipmaker. On Sunday, Huang admitted to having an affair. The media reported he was romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at the cloud company Megaspeed, who U.S. intelligence officials reportedly believe is a spy for China. Then, on Wednesday, the New York Times reported that Huang had a separate extramarital relationship with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of health care, who reports directly to him.
CNBC confirmed that the Nvidia board met Wednesday to discuss whether to remove Huang as CEO. U.S. lawmakers have also called for his resignation. Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993 and has led the company since.
Stacy Rasgon, a senior analyst at Bernstein, told CNBC’s “Squawk Box Asia” on Thursday that the relationship with a direct report changes the calculus for the board, saying it is “much tougher for the board to leave him in position.” Bernstein cut its Nvidia price target from $400 to $200 that same day.
Rasgon also questioned who could step in to replace Huang as the company’s strategic leader. “Jensen is Nvidia, right? He is the visionary. The reason they’re in the position that they’re in today is because he made decisions 10 years ago. Who is the right person now to make the decisions for the next 10 years? I don’t intuitively know who that person would be,” he said.
Richard Reyle, chief investment officer at Questar Capital Partners, told CNBC’s “Access Middle East” that Nvidia faces a difficult choice because it lacks a clear successor. “Nvidia doesn’t have that person in place, and so it’s going to be either a choice of keeping Jensen and being faced with months and months of being hauled in front of probably congressional boards and things to explain themselves and all kinds of interrogations,” Reyle said.
He added that ousting Huang would carry its own cost. “Or they’re going to have to move on from him, and that’s just going to make the stock uninvestable. That doesn’t make the AI trade uninvestable, but it certainly makes Nvidia uninvestable,” Reyle said.
Several Wall Street analysts told CNBC that removing Huang as CEO could send Nvidia shares down 20 percent.

72,2026-11-12,"Spanish parliament passes housing reforms limiting evictions but their duration will depend on upcoming snap election outcome.
Save
Share
The Spanish parliament has passed measures to limit evictions and real estate speculation in an attempt to make housing more affordable, a day after a nationwide general strike over the country’s housing crisis.
Spain’s interim lower house of parliament ratified the housing measures on Thursday; however, their duration will depend on the outcome of a snap election scheduled later in November.




The leading opposition party, the conservative Popular Party, has said that it will repeal the measures of the left-wing government if it takes power, which polls indicate could happen.
Large demonstrations over unaffordable housing swept the country in September when televised images of the eviction of 87-year-old Maria del Carmen Abascal, known as Maricarmen, from her home, shocked many Spaniards.
Protest camps sprang up in several cities, the largest in Madrid’s emblematic Puerta del Sol square. Maricarmen’s subsequent death in October caused national uproar, prompting Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez’s minority government to rush to present two decrees.
Sanchez called the general election, which had been scheduled for 2027, in response to the parliament’s failure to pass the decrees that included greater protection for tenants against evictions and the automatic renewal of tenant contracts.
The Popular Party and other conservative parties aligned to reject the measures, saying the laws would negatively affect the housing market, and proposed a building spree as the solution to the crisis.
On Wednesday, hundreds of thousands of Spaniards took to the streets in a 24-hour walkout over soaring rents and the high cost of living that the main unions called after the decrees were blocked.
Sanchez introduced another two decrees that were almost identical to the rejected ones. They were presented to a smaller parliamentary council on Thursday, giving the government a better chance of passing the laws.
The first decree passed in a 35–34 vote and includes a measure extending protections against evictions for tenants deemed by social services to be in a precarious financial or social position, as long as regional authorities cannot offer them an alternative residence.
Another measure attempted to reduce real estate speculation by stopping any company involved in the real estate market from buying properties for less than 70 percent of their value.
However, it was rejected 34-35 after the centre-right Basque party voted with the right-wing bloc after having supported the other decree, on grounds that the measure could lead homeowners to withdraw their properties from the rental market, triggering a drop in supply and even higher prices.

  By Steve Adams |
  at November 12, 2026 9:35am CDT  
The Guardians are planning to increase payroll for the 2027 season and have been exploring the market to add starting pitching, reports Zack Meisel of The Athletic. Jon Heyman of the New York Post, meanwhile, reports that Cleveland has some interest in left-handed reliever Tim Hill, who’s spent the past two and a half seasons with the Yankees but reached the open market at season’s end.
A payroll increase is a welcome development for Guards fans, though there’s really nowhere to go but up. Cleveland opened the 2026 season with a payroll around $75MM, and that’s before factoring in José Ramírez’s $10MM in deferred salary or the $6MM salary that closer Emmanuel Clase didn’t collect while spending time on the restricted list during the ongoing federal probe into his gambling/pitch-rigging scandal.
President of baseball operations Chris Antonetti didn’t provide a specific number to Meisel but did explicitly state payroll would increase. Much of that will be natural. Tanner Bibee’s five-year contract calls for his salary to jump from $4MM to $7MM.
More notably, Cleveland has a sizable 13-player arbitration class. Key players like Steven Kwan, Gavin Williams, Cade Smith, Brayan Rocchio and deadline pickup Jo Adell are due particularly significant raises. Kwan and Adell are projected by MLBTR contributor Matt Swartz to cost a combined $20.9MM. Williams, Smith and Rocchio are all arb-eligible for the first time. They’ll go from costing Cleveland under $3MM combined to a projected $13.4MM. Cleveland’s arb class has some non-tender candidates, but they’re all on the lower end of the pay scale. The 13-player group checks in at a combined $48.7MM.
Even with a few small-scale subtractions, that arb class is going to cost them more than $40MM, barring a trade. Antonetti has already downplayed the idea of trading Kwan, stating late last month that “as we sit here today, we’re expecting Steven Kwan to be a big part of a contending team next year.” Other clubs will surely inquire and try to change their minds, however, recognizing Cleveland’s perennially small payroll and frequent trades of players as they near the end of their club control window (e.g. Francisco Lindor, Corey Kluber, Josh Naylor).
With or without a notable trade from the arbitration class, Cleveland’s roster is organically becoming more expensive — even before any holes are addressed. Cleveland saw Foster Griffin, Nathaniel Lowe, Rhys Hoskins and Austin Hedges all reach free agency at season’s end. That creates some needs at first base and backup catcher, even if the organization expects top prospect Ralphy Velazquez to debut in 2027 and take most of the at-bats at first base, which is likely the case.
Most notably, Griffin’s departure opens a hole in a rotation that somewhat deceptively lacks depth. Cleveland managed to make it through the 2026 season with virtually no injuries on the starting staff. Williams, Bibee, Joey Cantillo and Rookie of the Year runner-up Parker Messick all made 30-plus starts. Slade Cecconi made 21 starts and gave way to Griffin when he was acquired at the deadline.
Making it through an entire season with not only nearly flawless health but also average-or-better results from all five members of a rotation is borderline impossible in today’s game. The fact that Cleveland was able to achieve that rarity in 2026 played a huge role in their division title, but no team can bank on that level of health and performance in two consecutive seasons.
The Guardians’ impeccable health in 2026 was crucial, given the surprising lack of MLB-ready depth in the upper minors. We’ve come to expect Cleveland will churn out quality pitchers on a near-annual basis, but injuries have depleted the top end of the Guardians’ crop of starting pitchers. Khal Stephen underwent UCL surgery this past season. Daniel Espino, once the top pitching prospect in the sport, was finally able to debut after missing nearly three full seasons due to injury, but he pitched all of 52 innings between Triple-A and the majors last year. It’s hard to know what to expect from him this year. Depth starters Logan Allen, Austin Peterson and Yorman Gomez all posted ERAs between 4.41 and 4.72 in Triple-A.
The rotation right now currently projects to include Williams, Messick, Bibee, Cantillo and Allen. The latter two are out of minor league options. Cantillo is locked into a spot, but Allen could be used in a swing role or could be traded or even jettisoned from the 40-man roster. Cleveland shouldn’t be expected to spend at the top of the market, but lower-cost veterans are abundant within this year’s free agent class and the trade market isn’t lacking for lower-salaried starters.
Meisel notes that Antonetti didn’t expressly rule out a reunion with Griffin, but his cost seems like it’ll probably be prohibitive. Even if he falls shy of the three-year, $39MM contract predicted by MLBTR on our annual Top 50 free agent rankings, Griffin seems like a lock to command a multi-year deal with an annual value north of $10MM. As MLBTR’s Contract Tracker shows, the only time Cleveland has signed a free agent starter to a multi-year deal in the past two decades is when they re-signed Shane Bieber a couple offseasons ago.
As for other needs, Heyman’s mention of Hill suggests an interest in bringing some cost-efficient relief help aboard. Whether that needs to be left-handed, specifically, isn’t clear just yet — but the team has already scooped up lefty Antoine Kelly on a minor league deal. Hill, 37 this winter, is coming off a down season that saw him record a 4.60 ERA in 58 2/3 innings. The soft-tossing side-armer yielded a career-worst 1.84 homers per nine frames with the Yankees in 2026. However, he’s the game’s premier ground-ball reliever (68.8% in ’26) and regularly avoids hard contact with his sidearm delivery and is all but impossible for opposing lefties to hit.
Meisel suggests Cleveland could also pursue a right-handed bat to mix in at first base and designated hitter. David Fry is on the roster as one such option but could be a non-tender candidate after hitting just .201/.313/.364 in 2026. Paul Goldschmidt is the top lefty-mashing first base/DH on the market who could be had in a part-time capacity and within Cleveland’s typical price range. The trade market could include Andrew Vaughn or change-of-scenery candidates like Mark Vientos or Jake Burger.

            POPULAR SEARCHES

LAS VEGAS — The second day of the MLB GM meetings at the Aria hotel gave us better insight into which teams are looking to be big movers and shakers in free agency. 
The day also gave us one of baseball's best offseason traditions – super agent Scott Boras talking up his clients in the way only he knows how. Here's what stood out: 
The Mets, Padres and Cubs are all looking to add multiple arms as they bulk up on starting pitching this offseason to help fill their desolate/empty/weak rotations. 
David Stearns, Mets president of baseball operations, said on Wednesday at the GM meetings that right-handers Nolan McLean and Christian Scott are the only two pitchers on the active roster whom he could commit to being in next year’s rotation. 
""I don’t know that there’s a specific number,"" Stearns explained. ""I’d say multiple. We talked yesterday about creating a complete staff and that’s 1 through 10, or 12. And so I think we’re going to be looking at all segments of the market, free agent, trade at the top end of the market and at the types of acquisitions that maybe don’t make a lot of headlines in November, December, January, but come June, July, August prove pretty impactful.""
Stearns added: ""I would expect there are going to be a member, or members, of our rotation who aren’t members of the organization right now.""
Beyond top free-agent starter Tarik Skubal, it’s a thin market for teams to pick from. 
The second-best starter on the market is veteran right-hander Freddy Peralta, who looked like a Cy Young-caliber ace in October for the Rays. His ""Fastball Freddy"" success and ensuing ALCS MVP honor came after an abbreviated stint with the Mets where we saw some of the worst performances of his career. It will be interesting to see if top contenders, like this trio of clubs who are desperate for starting pitching, will finally hand Peralta the nine-figure deal that seemed impossible to envision as recently as this past summer.  
Freddy Peralta is a name to watch in free agency this winter. (Photo by Mike Stobe/Getty Images) 
""There’s not a specific number that you can say ‘Alright, we have that many starters, we’ll be good,'"" Cubs GM Carter Hawkins said. ""I’ve seen teams, us included last year, say alright we’ve got 13 starters coming into spring training. We’ll be fine. Sure as heck, it’s like, alright, we need more. You can never have too many is obviously the saying you’re always going to hear. We’re going to look to find great deals on guys, find great pitchers, also. If we do that over and over the course of this offseason and build up our depth, we’ll be in a really good place.""
The Cubs lost all five starters who finished the 2026 season to free agency. That group includes Kevin Gausman, Matthew Boyd, Shota Imanaga, Clay Holmes and David Peterson. President of baseball operations Jed Hoyer is focused on beefing up the rotation and improving the overall quality of his pitching staff with savvy acquisitions this winter. It’s unclear if they’ll be big players at the top of the market for the best arms available.
The Padres, who lost all but one starter to free agency, are looking to move fast and shore up some rotation arms before the potential lockout next month.
 ""We’re going to need multiple pitchers,"" Padres president of baseball operations A.J. Preller said. ""So we understand that if we can line up I think we’ll be happy going into any, if there is a work stoppage type deal, we’ll be happy knowing we’ve got a fuller, complete roster than we have at this time."" — Deesha Thosar 
In the middle of his nearly hour-long address to reporters at the annual general managers meetings, agent Scott Boras’ phone rang. 
""Just another owner begging to meet with Skubal,"" Boras quipped. 
Tarik Skubal, the clear top free agent on the market who could soon become the highest-paid pitcher in MLB history, continues to dominate the early offseason discussion. In addition to the Dodgers and Mets, the Padres and Blue Jays are reportedly among the teams expected to meet with the star left-hander and his representation. 
""In the deep water is a talent treasure,"" Boras said. ""There’s no time to come up for air. So, teams have filled their tanks, and they’re all clearly ready for the Skubal dive.""
Tarik Skubal was this year's main subject of agent Scott Boras' quips (Getty). 
Boras did not say with certainty whether Skubal will sign before Dec. 1, when an anticipated lockout would freeze all transactions, but he intimated the likelihood of that happening. Before the last lockout in 2021, much of the activity — including the combined $500 million contracts with the Texas Rangers for Boras clients Corey Seager and Marcus Semien — occurred in the final hours and days before the sport temporarily shut down. 
This time, even more uncertainty looms beyond this month as MLB pushes for a salary cap that the players’ union vehemently rejects. On multiple occasions, Boras referenced how players feel ""emboldened"" by the success of the sport and its ""record franchise revenues in every quadrant of the market."" 
On one hand, the unsettled financial structure of the next labor agreement could make teams hesitant to add more long-term deals right now. On the other hand, the likelihood of a lockout could increase teams’ urgency to get a deal with Skubal done quickly while they still can. 
""I think they’re very concerned about losing the opportunity to sign select key players, and they’re pursuing it aggressively,"" Boras said, later adding that ""when we get what we believe is fair, we’re going to move."" 
Thus far, however, none of the top free agents have come off the board. The inactivity to this point opened the door for one of many Boras-isms during the session. 
""I think the anticipated offseason was sort of a bearish (beginning),"" Boras said. ""But it’s very clear that there is a dominant and strong Sku-bull market. I think it’s pretty clear teams are not devoid of pursuing their championship fortunes, and they understand there’s only one Tarik card.""  — Rowan Kavner
3.
As teams insist it’s ""business as usual"" at this year’s GM meetings, the threat of a lockout and an extended freeze loom after Dec. 1. 
And while there have been a few one-year deals handed out — pitchers Emilio Pagán and Jacob Waguespack to the Tigers, pitcher Ryan Weiss to the Padres, pitcher Robert Stock to the Mets — we have yet to see any of the top free agents sign multi-year deals. 
That’s not a huge surprise, given how early it is in the offseason. The GM meetings are typically a time to start conversations and lay the groundwork for deals rather than actually wrap them up. Still, it’s possible that the unpredictability of the terms of the next labor agreement could make teams — particularly those who may need to trim payroll, depending on the financial parameters of the CBA — more hesitant to add multi-year deals to the books. 
""I think every person is going to answer that a little bit differently,"" Red Sox chief baseball officer Craig Breslow told me. ""I can only speak to my experiences with the Red Sox, which is to say when there are opportunities to improve the team — I’ve gone to ownership and suggested that we pursue a player — I’ve had the support to do so."" 
""Every situation’s different,"" Blue Jays general manager Ross Atkins told me. ""We just consider them from every potential angle, and then depending on the level of impact, in some cases we’ll have to present that to ownership with some explanation for the potential of outcomes around that."" 
Dodgers general manager Brandon Gomes said all teams can do is function with what they know. And on his team, there simply aren’t many open spots available. They’ll look for a potential replacement for retired utility infielder Miguel Rojas and plan to be ""opportunistic"" in the bullpen market. Of course, a Skubal return is also a possibility. 
""So much of it is, what do we think is going to help us balance everything?"" Gomes told me. ""Now, in the future, what does that look like? What are the possibilities? We have to be as prepared as possible. The good thing for us is we have a really good team right now without doing anything. We feel like that is a championship caliber team. Are we ever satisfied with that? No."" — Kavner
4.
The Padres are among the teams expected to meet with Skubal, perhaps a sign that new owners José E. Feliciano and Kwanza Jones might be willing to increase payroll again for a franchise that had cut costs since former owner Peter Seidler’s passing after the 2023 season. 
Michael King is a top free-agent arm on the market. (Photo by Orlando Ramirez/Getty Images) 
""I think for us, the last three years has been about making our money work for us and finding some players who were hungry and might be able to overperform the contracts we gave them,"" said Padres president of baseball operations A.J. Preller. ""That part doesn’t change. You’re always looking for players you feel like are going to outperform what we’re paying them and guys that are going to go out and overachieve. But hopefully it opens up some other doors and avenues that we haven’t had the last three years. You don’t really know until you find out what the market says and what guys are looking for. That’s kind of what we’re doing right now.""
Preller said the new ownership group is ""committed to putting a winning team on the field,"" though he isn’t sure yet exactly how many more dollars he’ll be able to spend. That might depend on the player Preller is trying to add. 
The Padres certainly aren’t the favorites to win a bidding war for Skubal, considering the sizable contracts already on their books, the other competitors in the sweepstakes, and the fact that they need to replace nearly their entire rotation. 
The Padres and their new owners want to maximize the title window of Fernando Tatis Jr. and Manny Machado. (Photo by Orlando Ramirez/Getty Images) 
Michael King opted out of his deal, making him a free agent and one of the top arms available behind Skubal. The Padres declined their club option on Nick Pivetta, making him a free agent. Casey Mize, Robbie Ray and Walker Buehler are also free agents now, and a potential return in 2027 for Yu Darvish is still unknown. 
""We don’t need just one guy,"" Preller said. ""We need a group of guys that we feel like can throw a bunch of innings for us.""
Joe Musgrove and Randy Vásquez are the primary pitchers in the rotation. The Padres also added Ryan Weiss on a one-year deal for pitching depth. They remain open to bringing back King and Pivetta. 
Preller said the Padres are talking to King’s representatives ""every few days"" to check in and that there is mutual interest. 
""He’s let it be known he enjoyed his time in San Diego,"" Preller said. ""We’ll see if we can line up.""
Japanese slugger Teruaki Sato is on the Padres' radar. (Photo by Gene Wang - Capture At Media/Getty Images) 
It’s also possible that the Padres get involved in the bidding for the incoming crop of NPB talents. They would seemingly be fits for pitchers Kaima Taira or Hiromi Itoh, as well as top Japanese slugger Teruaki Sato, particularly if he moves to first base or a corner outfield spot. All three have already been posted, starting the clock for them to reach an agreement with an MLB team. 
""We’re aware,"" Preller said. ""We’ve been preparing."" — Kavner 
The Phillies were the worst defensive team in baseball with -51 Defensive Runs Saved this year, and the Mariners were not too far behind with -23 DRS, which ranked 26th in MLB. So it shouldn’t come as a surprise to hear their brass talk about how much these clubs will emphasize defense this offseason. 
Phillies president of baseball operations Dave Dombrowski is tasked with the delicate high-wire act of improving his offense, which ranked 22nd in MLB with a .707 OPS amid a deeply disappointing and inconsistent season, all while plugging in players who can pick it. 
Trea Turner led the majors with 21 errors. (Photo by Emilee Chinn/Getty Images) 
""We could easily find someone who could help us from the offensive perspective, but then we run into a problem if they wouldn’t give us the defense,"" Dombrowski said. ""And, as we’ve talked about for a while now, we’re emphasizing the defense."" 
How will Seattle go about it?
""We have to get better defensively,"" Mariners president of baseball operations Jerry Dipoto said. ""Our defense last year, in what was a year of struggle across almost all elements of our team, our defense was maybe our single biggest disappointment. It’s an area where we just have to get better, whether that’s in free agency or trade.""
Cal Raleigh had a down year for the Mariners. (Photo by Rob Leiter/MLB Photos via Getty Images) 
In addition to external acquisitions, Dipoto is banking on his ""young-ish, versatile team"" to move players around the diamond as part of his plan to improve the defense. In particular, the Mariners are aiming to improve the left side of their defense, and they will mix and match players to fix the problem. 
At the request of catcher Cal Raleigh, who asked the team to bring someone in who could specifically help work with the catchers on defense, the Mariners hired Tanner Swanson as their bench coach. Swanson, 44, came from the Yankees, where he spent the last seven seasons on their major-league coaching staff. 
""By and large, we just thought he’s the best catching instructor in the world,"" Dipoto said of Swanson. ""To be able to access that in addition to someone who has spent the last seven years dialed into what the Yankees do in terms of game strategy and preparation, and he’s sitting right next to Boony [Yankees manager Aaron Boone] and their bench coach year after year, and he’s the one bringing that gameplanning piece to the table. We thought that was a wonderful pairing with Jason Kanzler to provide our catchers with top-end instruction.""
Multiple reports indicate Seattle is moving on from shortstop J.P. Crawford, who spent eight seasons with the Mariners before he entered free agency this offseason, and they see infielder Colt Emerson at either third base or shortstop. The M’s would prefer to add a better defender at third or short to complement Emerson on the left side of the field. — Thosar"
76,2026-11-12,"

Heaping misery on top of the GPU crisis

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia is in turmoil after the resignation of Jensen Huang, with the CEO's departure coming after an accusation of him having an affair with a suspected Chinese spy (alongside an allegation of a second affair). Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress has been announced as the interim CEO to hold the Nvidia fort while the board conducts the search for a permanent replacement.
Team Green's stock has plunged during these revelations, and to say the loss of Huang is going to be felt keenly is an understatement. He was a key figure and intrinsically linked to Nvidia's success, a CEO cut from the same cloth as the likes of Steve Jobs.
He also exerted a level of control at Nvidia which was quite astonishing. Most CEOs have, what, 10 execs directly reporting to them (probably fewer). A few years back, Huang told the New York Times (via Business Insider) that he had around 50 direct reports in order to minimize the 'layers' in the company (meaning middle management).
Huang was also pivotal to Nvidia's entire business strategy, as analyst Ming-Chi Kuo made clear in a post on X, in terms of ""deciding on aggressive spec upgrades and making them happen"" based on his clout with the supply chain. Kuo observed (just prior to Huang's exit, I should clarify) that: ""His value is that he weighs strategy, organization, technology, and supply chain all at once, then makes the call quickly and puts resources behind it.""
Huang was clearly critical in terms of the internal workings of Nvidia as well as its broader strategy and direction. But with the CEO's departure, it's not just shareholders who should be worried – this could be the worst news for PC gamers who are already concerned about the state of play with GeForce GPUs.
 



Nvidia is now in a panic, let's make no bones about that. There was no clear succession path for the CEO position by all accounts, and this has all happened very suddenly.
The interim CEO, Colette Kress, is the CFO and as such a name readily associated with Nvidia's financial wherewithal. Presumably the idea is to introduce a sense of financial stability here – a steady hand on the rudder in that respect for the time being while the board searches for Huang's full-time replacement.
Sign up for breaking news, reviews, opinion, top tech deals, and more.
Whoever that turns out to be will doubtless feel that stepping into Huang's shoes – or maybe that should be donning his leather jacket – is an intimidating prospect.
So what's the worry for PC gamers specifically? Here's how the theory runs. Even with a new CEO – whoever that replacement may be – Nvidia obviously isn't going to suddenly run dry of profits. A Reuters report quotes Shay Boloor, chief market strategist at Futurum Equities, who speculated on Huang's departure before it happened, noting that: ""I don't see the next 12 to 18 months of revenue suddenly disappearing if Jensen were to leave.""
However, Boloor added: ""My bigger concern is next decade, because Jensen isn't simply ‌Nvidia's CEO ⁠but he's the person who has consistently identified where computing is going before customers even realise. Replacing that strategic vision ... is an enormous challenge.""
Here's where matters get tricky for gamers, as guess what? The CEO who eventually replaces Huang – and indeed Kress in the interim – is going to want to ensure big profits continue to be funnelled Nvidia's way, and so the obvious tactic is to try and maximize that AI revenue stream. In what's reportedly an internal message from Kress to Nvidia staff posted on X by Miranda Nazzaro of The Hill, the interim CEO said: ""We play a vital role in building the technology that is powering the AI revolution, and we have important work ahead of us.""
Gaming GPUs might become an even more sidelined consideration than they already are, due to concerns about impressing the board and shareholders, meaning Nvidia's new permanent CEO is probably going to go even more all-in on AI.
And let's not forget that part of the reason for GeForce being kept more central in Nvidia's overall messaging to date was due to Huang's personal leanings and fondness for the GPUs which he helped create at the turn of the last century. His successor won't be bound up in such sensitivities, and this GPU history will inevitably mean little to them.
 



Bringing this all into focus, Nvidia's GPUs (and AMD's) have already been hit by huge price increases of late. The high-end graphics cards have got truly out of hand with their pricing, and this situation won't get better. In fact, it was already predicted to get worse, and I've been saying for some time now that 2027 is going to be a dire year for GeForce GPUs (and Radeon for that matter – AMD has also fully declared itself as a 'data center first' company).
Now, heap on top of that already dismal outlook the loss of Huang – and an eventual replacement who is very likely to more consistently overlook consumer GPUs, to wholly focus on getting Nvidia's stock and revenue up by further capitalizing on AI – and you've got a prospective recipe for a frighteningly barren time for desktop GPUs in the future.
Nvidia is huge, a multi-trillion behemoth of a company. The world's second largest by market valuation (behind Apple). The AI sun is shining, and the hay to be made is endless (or it seems that way for now). Nvidia cannot afford to loosen its grip on the huge revenue streams it has built up, and with Huang gone, make no mistake – all hands will be on deck to ensure that the data center chips keep flowing. As for gaming GPU chips, well, the GeForce brand could now be more firmly on the path to becoming a thing of the past.
Maybe that's overly dramatic, granted, but I can't help feeling that it's inevitable that the stock levels and therefore pricing of Nvidia's consumer GPUs won't be helped by Huang's exit. And frankly, the last thing we need is for yet another negative drag factor to hit PC graphics cards as we head into the murky future of GPUs.

77,2026-11-12,"Flooded tents and high prices leave Gaza families struggling to keep children warm as winter approaches.
Save
Share
Gaza City, Gaza Strip – At night, as temperatures drop inside their makeshift shelter in Gaza City, Amira Saad struggles to keep her four-month-old baby, Mohammed, warm.
The baby mostly only has thin summer clothes. Unable to afford winter clothing, 27-year-old Amira has borrowed a warm garment from a neighbour, but remains worried about the cold during the night.
She lives with 18 family members, including nine children, under a tarp offering little protection from the elements. Finding enough warm clothes and blankets for everyone has become increasingly difficult.
The family was displaced from Gaza City’s Shujayea neighbourhood earlier in Israel’s genocidal war on Gaza, and now lives in a makeshift camp west of the city.
Recent heavy rain has flooded their shelter, soaking mattresses, pillows, and blankets.
“We were flooded while we were sleeping. The water came inside and soaked our blankets, mattresses and pillows. Everything was wet,” Amira says.
The children mostly have old, torn summer clothes. Amira’s mother-in-law, 60-year-old Basima Saad, is also worried about her own 23-year-old daughter, who has a disability, and needs warm clothes and medication.
“Her hands become as cold as ice. I keep her beside me in bed to warm her, just as I would a small child,” Basima says. “I dress her before I dress myself. I have to make sure she is warm first.”
With no regular income, the family relies on community kitchens for food. Spare money for winter clothes and blankets simply is not available.
“My husband does not have a job. Two days ago, he went out to work breaking up rubble, but he broke his hand. The situation is very bad, and the cold is extremely severe,” Amira says.
The danger to the Palestinians living in these conditions, and particularly the very young, is not hypothetical.
On Thursday, a nine-month-old baby, Kinan Ahmed Raqiq, died of hypothermia. His father told AFP that he woke up in his tent to find that his son’s skin had turned blue.
Tragedies like that make Amal al-Maydana, a 46-year-old other of seven, fearful.
She’s raising her children alone – her husband Hamza was killed by an Israeli quadcopter in January 2025, and her eldest son Haitham was killed by a sniper in November of the same year, age 25 at the time.
She is originally from Gaza City’s Zeitoun neighbourhood and has been displaced six times during the war, fleeing Israeli attacks and losing the family’s home, land, and car.
Now living in a tent, Amal struggles to provide for her children. Her younger daughters, Rimas and Malak, are still wearing summer clothes despite the arrival of winter.
“Everything is expensive… children’s clothes, shoes, everything,” she says.
Amal tries to make do with what is available, but it is not enough to meet her daughters’ needs.
Rain also seeps through the damaged tarpaulins covering the family’s tent. With no heating equipment, they rely on blankets to keep warm.
“We have no means of heating ourselves; we just cover the children with blankets,” she says.
Amal fears another storm will bring more rain and cold. “We just pray that God makes this winter easier for the people living in tents,” she says.
The United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA) says 94 percent of Gaza’s population urgently needs shelter assistance. Some 65 percent of households live in tents or makeshift shelters, while 650,000 children need winter aid, including warm clothing and blankets.
By September, aid agencies had secured enough winter clothing for just 9,000 households and bedding for 50,000 – far short of what was needed. OCHA said funding shortages were further hampering preparations for winter.
The UN Children’s Fund (UNICEF) has warned that winter conditions put children in Gaza at increased risk of hypothermia and respiratory infections – at least 11 children died from hypothermia last winter, according to the Gaza Ministry of Health. The agency is seeking $97.5m for its 2026-2027 winter response, which aims to support 540,000 children and their families across Gaza and the occupied West Bank.
Recent rains have worsened the crisis. UNICEF said on November 9 that flooding had affected more than 106,000 people, while some 950,000 displaced Palestinians were living in flood-prone areas.
More than three years into Israel’s genocidal war, which has killed more than 74,600 Palestinians and wounded over 176,000, homes and livelihoods across Gaza have been devastated. Nearly two million people have been displaced, many repeatedly, and hundreds of thousands remain in tents and damaged shelters.
As the war enters its fourth year, unemployment and rising prices have made basic necessities increasingly unaffordable, leaving families struggling to protect their children from the cold.
In Gaza City’s Rimal market, 25-year-old shop owner Mohammed Abu Shanar sees the same struggle among his customers. Many ask about winter clothing prices, but leave without buying anything.
“The prices are very high. They are not within everyone’s reach. People come in, ask how much something costs, and then leave because they cannot afford it,” he says.
Mohammed sells around four to six winter tracksuits a day. He says many children from nearby displacement camps still wear short-sleeved or lightweight clothes despite the cold.
He attributes the high prices to shortages of goods entering Gaza – the result of continued Israeli restrictions – and the cost of buying stock from wholesalers.
“First, there is a shortage of goods. The wholesalers hold on to their prices, and the merchandise is expensive for us, too. If these items were selling for 20 shekels ($6.25) or 25 shekels ($7.80) instead of 40 shekels ($12.60) or 50 shekels ($15.70), perhaps people would be able to buy them,” he says.
Mohammed says he has invested more than 20,000 shekels (about $6,265) in winter stock, but after three weeks of sales he has recovered less than 5,000 shekels (about $1,570).
People like Anas Hamad simply don’t have the money to buy from traders like Mohammed.
Anas and his family are displaced from Beit Hanoun in northern Gaza. Before the war, he ran a supermarket employing his four brothers. The war destroyed their homes and business, leaving all five university graduates without work.
With no income and unable to afford new clothes, Anas relies on second-hand markets and hand-me-downs to dress his three children.
He recalls how his five-year-old daughter, Jana, asked him for a new shirt. He could only point to the one she was wearing, which had belonged to her brother.
The family has lived in their badly torn tent for around two years. Despite his wife’s efforts to sew and patch it, rainwater enters from above and below. During the recent downpour, their bedding and belongings were soaked.
Anas says his three-year-old child was trembling from the cold, while the sound of rain hitting the tent frightened the children.
“I have to choose between buying food for my children, getting them warm clothes and blankets, or buying tarpaulins to protect our tent from the coming rain,” he says. “I don’t know what to prioritise or what to leave behind.”

78,2026-11-12,"In the summer of 1942, a cash-strapped William Faulkner signed on to work at Warner Bros. as a screenwriter, earning three hundred dollars a week. His first task was to whip up a biographical treatment of General Charles de Gaulle, leading the Free French government from exile in London. Provisionally titled “The De Gaulle Story,” the project was intended as hagiography—explicitly so, as President Franklin Roosevelt had proposed the idea to Jack Warner, hoping to boost de Gaulle’s credibility with the American public. Faulkner gave the gig his best shot, producing some twelve hundred pages and entertaining fussy notes from French consultants. (A memo titled “Observations on Inexact Details” cautioned, “In a cafe in France one does not play dominos but cards or billiard.”) Ultimately, Faulkner found his protagonist so hard to pin down that he purged the bio from the bio-pic, instructing a colleague, “Let’s dispense with General de Gaulle as a living character in the story.” The script was soon relegated to oblivion.
Churchills and Roosevelts—not to mention Hitlers—abound in cinema, but, even in France, bringing de Gaulle to the big screen has historically been a fraught enterprise. As the British historian Julian Jackson has written, postwar filmmakers strove to emphasize the collective nature of resistance rather than focussing on a single great man; yet, when de Gaulle returned to power in 1958, becoming President after more than a decade’s retirement from politics, it was with such command that, as Jackson puts it, “to dare to ask an actor to portray de Gaulle himself would have been an act of lèse-majesté.” (The director Réné Clément solved the problem by using archival footage whenever the General’s presence was required.) Pierre Schoendoerffer and Bertrand Tavernier both tried to make de Gaulle films and failed, the latter surely unhelped by his producer’s insistence that the lead character be played by Gérard Depardieu. Then there was the challenge of de Gaulle’s distinctive physiognomy. Such were the sensitivities surrounding him and his myth that a single actor—the towering, schnozzy, serviceably chinless Adrien Cayla-Legrand—maintained an effective monopoly on the role for decades. It wasn’t until 2020 that the first full-scale feature film devoted to the General, Gabriel Le Bomin’s “De Gaulle,” premièred in French theatres. It closed just ten days later, with COVID-19 advancing like the Panzerjäger-Abteilung 187.
This is all to say that Antonin Baudry knew what he was getting into when he signed on to co-write and direct the most ambitious, improbable de Gaulle movie yet: “La Bataille de Gaulle”—a two-part, five-plus-hour epic, based on Julian Jackson’s nine-hundred-page biography “A Certain Idea of France.” (The book's title alludes to the opening lines of de Gaulle’s own memoirs: “All my life, I have had a certain idea of France.”) The film captivated France this summer, and the first part opened last week in the U.S. Its timing has certainly been fortuitous, with France facing a Presidential election that may put the far right in power for the first time since the Vichy regime. But when we met recently, at a café in the Luxembourg Gardens, Baudry told me he believes that the film has resonated largely because of its human element. He looked chicly rumpled in cream trousers, a button-down, and white leather high-tops decorated with red lightning bolts. He said, of the filmmakers who preceded him, “I understood the malediction—none of them had dared and managed to crack the character.”
Baudry, who is fifty-one, began his career far from the moving image, studying math before earning degrees in engineering and literature from France’s arch-prestigious grandes écoles. (He managed to slip in a film-studies minor, with a thesis on Wong Kar-wai.) After graduating, he entered the French diplomatic corps, where he worked as a speechwriter to the Foreign Minister at the time, Dominique de Villepin, and at least partially authored his famous speech at the United Nations, opposing the Iraq War. After that, he pseudonymously co-wrote a graphic novel called “Quai d’Orsay,” after the seat of the foreign ministry. The book—a juicy look at the inner workings of foreign-policymaking—was a hit, selling more than sixty thousand copies of its first edition. Having proved himself as a sort of beast of the French establishment, devouring the competition in every arena he entered, Baudry volunteered to co-write a film adaptation. Then he decided he wanted to be a director. Casual. So he wangled a meeting with Jérôme Seydoux, the chairman of Pathé film company, and persuaded him to finance a nuclear-submarine thriller called “Chant du Loup,” which premièred in 2019 to warm reviews and respectable takings.
For the de Gaulle project, Pathé gave Baudry carte blanche and an initial seventy-five million euros, an unheard-of budget for a French production. (The film was called “La Bataille de Gaulle” in France but is known as “De Gaulle” in the U.S.) It focusses on the critical years of 1940 to 1944, beginning, in Part 1, with de Gaulle refusing to join the collaborationist Vichy government and ending, in Part 2, with his triumphant return from exile during the Liberation of Paris. Baudry had a breakthrough when he and his co-writer, Bérénice Vila, who is also his wife, started thinking about de Gaulle as a figure spiritually not of the mid-century but of the Middle Ages. “I realized that de Gaulle was a modern Don Quixote,” Baudry recalled, citing the General’s “denial, his stubbornness, his readiness to die rather than admitting the unbearable reality that France had capitulated.” Once the notion of de Gaulle as a knight-errant had clicked, Baudry and Vila came up with a tactic to alleviate the pressure of his legend: as they wrote, they called their hero “Robert” instead of “Charles.” They emphasized the initial lostness of his cause: a mad band of marginals, holding on to France out of unaccountable faith, rather than a reasonable chance of wresting control from Vichy. By that point, Baudry recalled, “I had my character in my veins, and I was going to make these films.”
For the title role, Baudry cast Simon Abkarian, an actor best known for his work on the stage. The film took a hundred and fifty gruelling days to shoot, with reports of delays, quarrels, and a generally trying atmosphere leaking from sets in Paris and Cherbourg. Several months into filming, some twenty crew members quit, including the first assistant director and the director of photography. After filming finally wrapped, in 2024, post-production took more than a year, and Part 1 missed an anticipated release date in 2025. When it finally premièred, in May, at Cannes, reviews were mostly positive. Les Cahiers du Cinema criticized its formal “heavy-handedness” and its “idealized vision” of France, but critics in Le Monde and Le Figaro loved it. “De Gaulle” had won a battle, but it had not won the war.
The first installment hit French theatres in June, to dismal ticket sales. Baudry, meanwhile, was M.I.A., still editing the sound for Part 2. “I honestly didn’t give a shit about the noise outside the mixing room,” he recalled. The film’s producers, hoping for a turnaround, moved up the release of the second installment to coincide with la Fête du Cinema, a nationwide promotion offering discounted tickets. (The films’ full titles—“La Bataille de Gaulle: L’Âge de Fer” and “La Bataille de Gaulle: J’Écris Ton Nom”—were unwieldy enough to cause at least one moviegoer, yours truly, to accidentally see the second part first.) Executives overhauled the marketing campaign. Seydoux told me, “We had a crisis meeting, and I said, ‘We have a rifle with only two shots.’ ”
Then came a third, lucky blast—record-breaking heat, which sent people flocking to the cinema, one of the only reliable places to find air-conditioning. By its fourth week in theatres, “La Bataille de Gaulle” had seen admissions surge by seventy per cent. Word-of-mouth took over, and, suddenly, “La Bataille de Gaulle” was a sensation. I heard chatter about it, in the course of a few days, from both a government minister and my boxing coach. “It’s French panache!” Le Parisien crowed, heralding “a fiasco turned triumph.” The trajectory was almost embarrassingly de Gaullian: a crazy project, flailing and discounted, mounting a gritty comeback that would subsequently be treated as inevitable.
In late September, Jérôme Seydoux was sitting in an orange chair at a huge circular conference table on the top floor of Pathé Palace, his company’s Paris headquarters. He has piercing blue eyes and the plain confidence of a man born to industrial wealth—his mother was a member of the Schlumberger oil dynasty—who managed to conquer another field completely. (He is also grandfather to the actor Léa Seydoux.) At ninety-two, he is something of a cross between Louis B. Mayer and Willy Wonka, confecting France’s major films according to a proprietary formula of market savvy and personal conviction.
Seydoux and Baudry like to say that the “De Gaulle” project took shape out of serendipity: over lunch, they discovered that they were both reading the Julian Jackson biography. But in a way Seydoux has been preparing for much longer to make such a film. “I am an old man, and the story, to some extent, is the story of my life,” he admitted. Seydoux witnessed firsthand the Liberation of Paris, in August of 1944. “We tried to go to the Champs-Élysées, but we didn’t make it, because we were crossing the Tuileries and there were people shooting on the Rue de Rivoli,” he recalled. “The soldiers advised my mother to have us all go under a truck to be protected.” Another day, he and his sister were forbidden to go outside, but they snuck out and saw a German tank. “Kids were able to move because nobody would stop them,” he said.
Making a wildly expensive diptych about a long-dead, famously wooden statesman was a gamble, surely, but it conformed to a strategy that Seydoux has pursued to success in recent years,. This involves taking canonical French stories and turning them into “grandes fresques historiques,” the kind of lavish historical epics at which Hollywood once excelled in the nineteen-fifties and sixties. The movies are old-fashioned, and they are expensive, but they have proved adept at drawing big, cross-generational crowds to physical theatres. Pathé’s “The Three Musketeers: D’Artagnan” was the third-highest-grossing French film of 2023; the next year, its “The Count of Monte Cristo” took second place. “It’s this sort of instinctive I.P.,” the film critic Jordan Mintzer told me. “We have Marvel. They have Dumas.” The advantage of resurrecting a wartime superhero is that the world he saves is our own, though “De Gaulle” would have been a very different proposition if it had followed its protagonist into the years of the Algerian War, decolonization, and student revolt.
Baudry has said that he approached the film from another kind of decolonial perspective—that of a French director “breaking the Atlanticist taboo” and liberating his national history from the clutch of the American cultural empire. He spent years living in the U.S. (where he created Albertine, the French cultural center in Manhattan), and when he came back to France he found “a country that was mentally colonized,” as he told Le Monde. “I saw advertisements in public spaces written in broken English that even Americans wouldn’t have understood.” His de Gaulle is difficult, not because he is a comedy Frenchman but because he is a steadfast believer in the near-hopeless cause of maintaining the sovereignty of la Fraaaaaahnce. (Abkarian’s enunciation is the stuff of epic earworms.) “I embody everything the Americans detest,” de Gaulle once said. “I am weak, and I stand my ground.” In the film, he also says borderline delusional stuff such as, “Mosquitoes don’t bite General de Gaulle!” In the next scene, he’s in bed with malaria. Baudry explained, of the General’s almost mystical self-belief, “You have to be passionate. If you’re reasonable, you leave.”
Roosevelt, played by Campbell Scott, is a far more sour, transactional figure than the morally driven humanitarian we know from American history books. Churchill (Simon Russell Beale) waffles and feints as much as he blusters. Baudry told me, “This is the first time we’re seeing this full history through own our lens.” Call it a reverse Pepé Le Pew, flattening Anglo-American icons to give some space not only to de Gaulle but to his internal antagonists and allies and to the French people themselves. Niels Schneider’s sensational performance makes you long for a spinoff two-part, five-hour bio-pic of the Free French general Philippe Leclerc. Baudry’s dramatization of the Germans' violent crackdown on dozens of mostly young, civilian protesters on November 11, 1940, incorporating real live archival footage, is among the most moving set pieces I’ve seen. And nobody who has seen this film will ever forget the Battle of Bir-Hakeim.
There are many ironies to “De Gaulle”—a great-man bio-pic distinguished by its ensemble cast; an anti-American, or at least American-agnostic, film done in high Hollywood style. But its biggest contradiction might be the fact that it is both avowedly antifascist and profoundly patriotic, a film about resistance that could be seen as almost reactionary in its insistence on the purity of de Gaulle and his cause. During our coffee, I asked Baudry whether he’d had any reservations about making a movie that is so overtly, even unfashionably proud of France. He answered in a sidelong way, saying that he hadn’t anticipated the powerful frissons of pride that many moviegoers had reported feeling. Weeks later, I received a long text: “I keep thinking about our exchange in the Luxembourg Garden,” it began. “I don’t want to sound naïve on this,” Baudry wrote. Part of the problem, he said, was that “the far-right has managed to monopolize patriotism,” confiscating “our flag and our pride.” He brought up American exceptionalism, and said that “De Gaulle” does not try to argue for a French version. “It doesn’t say that the French are better than the Spanish, the British, the Americans,” he said. “It just shows people trying to save their country which is France and which they love.”
“Why now?” is an irksome question to impose on an artist, but with Europe struggling to assert itself against an increasingly aggressive American hegemon, and people all over the world searching for models of resistance against autocratic regimes, Baudry is not oblivious to the film’s broader resonances. “I thought to myself, It’s Trump-Zelensky!” he told Le Grand Continent, of a scene in which Roosevelt and de Gaulle face off at the Anfa Hotel, in Casablanca, in 1943. Baudry told me that the current political situation put him in mind of the words of Edward Grey, the British Foreign Secretary in 1914: “The lamps are going out all over Europe, we shall not see them lit again in our life-time.” He continued, “That was true, and that’s a little bit what we’re fearing now.” “De Gaulle” joins a slew of other recent French movies about the Second World War, including “Notre Salut,” a drama by Emmanuel Marre about his collaborationist great-grandfather, and a starry bio-pic of the French Resistance leader Jean Moulin. But “De Gaulle,” like its hero, stands out for its willingness to be true to itself, and even a little weird. Baudry brought up Don Quixote again. “A knight devoted to his lady isn’t necessarily convinced that she is technically more beautiful than, say, the king’s wife,” he told me. “He just thinks she’s perfect, and he’ll die for her any time if needed.” It’s a certain idea about a certain idea. ♦
