79,2026-11-12,"In 1956, an ancient gentleman named Samuel James Seymour appeared on a game show called “I’ve Got a Secret,” where he revealed, in a small voice weakened by a recent fall, that he was likely the last living witness to the assassination of Abraham Lincoln. The blown-out kinescope transmission of the episode, preserved on YouTube, is a wormhole—a shortcut through the space-time continuum. The viewer takes this shortcut via her laptop or phone, through a television program aired within living memory, featuring a guest who entered adulthood not yet knowing cars or planes or skyscrapers or light bulbs, and whose own father was born before germ theory and the sewing machine.
I often feel as though I am shuffling onto the stage of my children’s education in the manner of Mr. Seymour. On one side of the wormhole is the stuff of the public K-12 schools I attended in the nineteen-eighties and nineties: chalkboards and Scantrons, film strips and secondhand Signet Classics, and big, battered textbooks in grocery-bag slipcovers, passed down from student to student, year upon year. On the other side of the wormhole are my kids’ Chromebooks, pre-loaded with Google’s suite of education software and A.I. tools, along with apps from myriad other companies. I cup my hand to my ear and ask the kids to speak up as they recite the gamified learning platforms they have known: Wayground (né Quizizz), BrainPOP, ABCya!, Epic!, Kahoot!. My son, who is nine, does homework games on the free version of a cartoon show called 99math, but he’d like to join the app’s “Premium Club,” whose members can “Play UNLIMITED GAMES at home,” “Collect RARE EGGS and get EPIC PETS,” and “Get EPIC OUTFITS for your avatar,” all for just $9.99 a month.
My daughter, age eleven, spends a chunk of every social-studies class on Blooket, where she earns tokens by taking multiple-choice quizzes arranged by subject and grade level. (A recent Veterans Day–themed Blooket quiz led off with “Veterans give up a lot to serve their countries and show a lot of _____.” The correct answer is patriotism.) Using her tokens, she has assembled an army of cartoon avatars known as Blooks; her most prized platoon of late is her “Spooky Pack” of ten Blooks—a googly-eyed creature, a creepy caramel apple—who look like extras from a factory-outlet remake of “Monsters, Inc.” She acquired them ahead of Halloween by answering sub-grade-level questions about the water cycle. I bleat hoarsely at her through the wormhole, demanding to know how the avatars and the coins and all the clicking help her learn anything. “This is the not-learning part,” she explains, gently. I feel my dentures loosening as I wander away.
On my own computer, I click around in hapless search of a university-level tutorial or peer-reviewed study that might explain the cognitive benefits of Zorblitz or Pirate Pool or Fishing Frenzy (three of Blooket’s many game modes). Instead, I stumble on a promotional video for yet another platform familiar to my kids—a program called Boost Reading. In Boost, instead of clicking answers in order to earn Blooks, you click answers in order to earn accessories for avatars known as Curiosos. “The kids are really engaged,” a literacy coach murmurs despondently in the video, over a closeup of a student wearing headphones and staring at a screen. A fourth grader tells the camera, “I feel as if I’m not even reading. I feel as if I’m just playing a game.” She means it as a compliment. Some ersatz Philip Glass tinkles in the background, the sad-anxious sound of a mental-health P.S.A., or of your parents’ divorce, or of an entire nation sliding into an oddly Pavlovian illiteracy.
Boost Reading is one of the many products offered by the ed-tech company Amplify Education, which is backed by hundreds of millions of dollars in venture capital. These V.C. firms include Learn Capital, which, according to its website, focusses on “AI-powered learning, digital education, workforce training, and career advancement”; and A-Street Ventures, which centers “digital-first instructional materials in curriculum and new paradigms for student assessment.” In New York City Public Schools—the largest system in the country, with nearly nine hundred thousand students and an annual budget of about forty-five billion dollars—Amplify has been a major presence for more than fifteen years. Joel Klein, who was schools chancellor under Mayor Michael Bloomberg and a key national force behind the reorientation of public schools around ed-tech, later spent several years as chief executive of Amplify. In fiscal year 2026, Amplify received $12.4 million in contractual payments from the city’s Department of Education, including for curricula in math, science, and English Language Arts (E.L.A.).
The ed-tech that has swallowed our public schools is overwhelmingly driven by private capital, and Amplify is just one example. In fiscal year 2026, New York City paid contracts totalling $25.6 million to Curriculum Associates, whose majority stakeholders are private-equity firms. The company’s flagship product is i-Ready, a reading-and-math-assessment software platform that has kept its foothold in New York City and across the country despite being universally reviled by students, parents, and teachers. Curriculum Associates is facing multiple legal challenges related to i-Ready, including a class-action lawsuit, filed in the District of Massachusetts, alleging violations of consumer-protection and privacy laws; and a false-advertising case, filed in D.C. Superior Court, brought by an education nonprofit. The latter suit alleges that i-Ready “does not tell a teacher what question a child missed or why they missed it”; that the software promises “personalized instruction” but instead “runs students with opposite needs through the same pre-built lessons”; and that it measures students’ progress “on Curriculum Associates’ own test of its own lessons, scored by Curriculum Associates—a closed loop its marketing nowhere discloses.” (Curriculum Associates has denied any wrongdoing.)
Public schools would seem an odd fit for private equity, which is typically premised on a strategy of slashing costs, trimming head count, and finding the right moment to sell up. Yet Heggerty, which provides literacy curricula to some New York City schools, is backed by private equity. The majority owner of Really Great Reading (E.L.A. software) is private equity. The sole owner of Savvas (software for multiple subjects) is private equity. And one of the D.O.E.’s top curriculum venders, H.M.H.—a descendant of Houghton Mifflin, a name synonymous with old-school textbooks—is owned by the private-equity firm Veritas Capital. H.M.H. and its subsidiary N.W.E.A. have a total of nine approved learning programs in New York, including the E.L.A. curricula “Into Reading” and “Into Literature,” which are used in two-thirds of the city’s districts. In fiscal year 2026, the D.O.E. made payments exceeding twenty million dollars to H.M.H.
Veritas Capital, which has significant A.I.-related investments, refers to H.M.H. on its website as an “adaptive learning company,” though H.M.H.’s own site is more explicit on this point: “Only HMH puts AI intelligence inside the high-quality instructional materials—by design, not an add-on.” This is a surprising bona fide for the most prominent curriculum provider in New York City, where, in response to intense anti-A.I. public sentiment, Mayor Zohran Mamdani and his schools chancellor, Kamar Samuels, recently announced a one-year moratorium on A.I. for students in eighth grade and under, along with recommendations on reduced screen time.
Private capital’s entanglement with increasingly A.I.-driven ed-tech places it at cross-purposes with a growing number of public-school advocates. The D.O.E. has imminent plans to launch five A.I. pilot programs for high-school students, in math, writing, and A.I. literacy; two of the pilots are from nonprofits that have received significant funding from Google, a third is from a company backed by venture capital, and a fourth is from Intel, whose top shareholders include the A.I. behemoth Nvidia. But the AIM Coalition—the umbrella organization of teachers, families, and advocates that successfully agitated for the A.I. moratorium—is militating against the pilots, arguing that they have no proven educational benefits, that they may harm teen-agers’ cognitive development, and that they effectively compel teachers and students to supply A.I. companies with free labor and personal data.
The incursion of A.I into classrooms is so recent, so freshly strange, that it may yet be possible for advocates to unwind it further—and perhaps unwind a lot of unwanted and pernicious ed-tech along with it. But it is also terribly strange that so many avatars of venture capital and private equity have been permitted to financialize public schools, which are neither designed nor intended to create profits or customers. It’s stranger still that these investors see so many dollar signs in a system such as New York City’s, where, according to the D.O.E.’s estimations, more than seventy per cent of students live in poverty.
Public schools have been providing private companies with straightforward revenue streams for generations; the outfit now known as H.M.H., for example, has existed in one form or another since the early nineteenth century. In the olden days, though, a school in the market for curricula could simply offer money to a publisher in exchange for its textbooks, then use them until they fell apart. The publisher was not granted access to the personal information or schoolwork or grades of the children using the books, as is often the case with Google and Microsoft’s learning-management platforms. These purchases did not put the school on the hook for future software or tech upgrades. And, unlike apps, books do not disappear overnight if a school does not renew its subscription to them.
The technologization of learning, in short, introduced elements of technofeudalism into the relationship between schools and ed-tech companies, creating far more opportunities for monetizing students by harvesting their data. And no company plays the part of feudal lord better than Google, which earlier this year foisted its Gemini suite of A.I. tools on public schools, much to the surprise and dismay of many educators and families. Last week, Google announced that early next year, by popular demand, it would permit schools to turn off A.I. features in its K-12 software, but that schools choosing to do so would also lose access to Google Search—a trade-off that carried a whiff of extortion. Of course, students in search of information can go directly to the URLs of trusted sources or use an alternative search engine such as DuckDuckGo, as Naveed Hasan, a computer scientist who has two children in public schools in Manhattan, told me. “This is actually a great opportunity to say, ‘Hey, we don’t need most of this Google stuff,’ ” he said. “Somehow they’re trying to blackmail us with a free product. Like, who cares?”
Technofeudalism also perpetuates itself in the form of the computer-based assessment. Though standardized tests—preparing for them, administering them—have been eroding classroom-instruction time across the country since the signing of the No Child Left Behind Act, in 2002, their corrosive effects intensified in many districts after COVID. In 2021, as schools in New York City were reopening for full-time, in-person learning, the D.O.E. began mandating triannual online assessments in reading and math. These new tests, intended to measure pandemic-driven learning loss, were sourced from MAP Growth, which is developed by N.W.E.A.; i-Ready, from Curriculum Associates; and Acadience Learning, whose parent company is backed by private equity. “The logic was, ‘We need to identify the gaps if we’re going to close them,’ ” Kyle DeAngelis, who is a third-grade teacher at a public school in Harlem, told me. “That made sense after a global-health meltdown. But, six years later, we’re still doing the same things, and schools are not getting the resources they need to close those gaps.”
Hasan is one of five elected parent representatives to the Panel for Educational Policy, the governing body that helps determine rules and budgets for the D.O.E. “All of our curriculum now is based on test scores,” he told me. “The standardized tests don’t demand long-form reading or writing, and therefore the kids aren’t taught to have the endurance to read a book.” Only about fifteen per cent of public schools in New York City currently employ a librarian. “They have mandated a curriculum with basically no books in it,” Hasan said. “We don’t have libraries and librarians because there’s no test to see if your kid is good at library.”
So far, the devil’s bargain of exchanging books for test scores appears to be a one-way transaction. According to the most recent test data from the National Assessment of Educational Progress, the reading scores of both fourth and eighth graders in New York State have dropped nine points in the past decade. Those results are especially bleak when you consider that the tests don’t measure for a student’s reading stamina, or quantify the joy and excitement a young person might feel in reading a book for fun, as opposed to scanning an excerpt in order to extract the correct answer and maybe a prize of some kind: a few digital coins, a new hat for her avatar, a robot’s automated words of praise.
Given the expense and inefficacy of ed-tech, the D.O.E.’s outsourcing of curricula is especially puzzling to many teachers and parent advocates. “Why are we reaching out to private industry for what should be core public responsibilities?” Hasan asked. “Curriculum design should be done in-house, by teachers who know their local communities. Otherwise, the priorities will be set by the venders.” Hasan and other advocates singled out H.M.H.’s “Into Reading” program—which is widely disliked for its reliance on excerpts, lack of diverse authors, and flat, voiceless content—as illustrative of the need for local curriculum design.
DeAngelis, who has a master’s degree in curriculum and teaching, said he was concerned that H.M.H.-style heat-and-serve learning may have a de-skilling effect on his youngest colleagues. “With the proliferation of these prepackaged curricula, I fear we are raising a generation of teachers who believe that teaching is downloading a lesson and just delivering whatever is in there,” he told me. De-skilling is likewise a concern in any school system, including New York’s, where inexperienced teachers are permitted or even encouraged to use A.I.—arguably a form of cognitive outsourcing—to generate lesson plans, classroom presentations, and feedback on student work. Martina Meijer, who teaches fourth grade in Brooklyn, told me that teachers are frequently receiving A.I.-written feedback from their supervisors. “It’s the same quality of work that a middle-school teacher would get from a student essay written by A.I.,” she said.
Meijer and DeAngelis are both part of the Movement of Rank-and-File Educators (MORE), which is the social-justice caucus of the United Federation of Teachers and a member group in the AIM Coalition. MORE-U.F.T.’s adversarial approach to ed-tech and A.I. in schools diverges sharply from the incautious optimism of the mainline U.F.T., which represents most public school teachers in New York City. Last year, the U.F.T. and its counterpart union, the American Federation of Teachers, launched the National Academy for AI Instruction, a training program that is funded by a twenty-three-million-dollar partnership with Microsoft, OpenAI, and Anthropic. The academy’s classes and online resources offer guidance on how teachers can harness A.I. to lighten their workloads; in one course, teachers can “practice crafting AI prompts specifically for English learners,” and, in another, they can “learn how to build and personalize AI agents that act as instructional aides, planners, or feedback partners using educator-friendly AI tools.”
In accepting A.I. money for A.I. purposes, the teachers’ unions seem to be instantiating an idea that many of its rank-and-file members reject: that A.I. and ed-tech writ large can aid and enhance the work of teachers, as opposed to eroding or supplanting their role. “There’s no analysis of the labor issues that are raised by accepting corporate curricula and the rolling out of A.I. to address unacceptable labor conditions,” Meijer told me. “If the situation is that teachers are training computers to do their job for them, then we are at a very weak position at the bargaining table.”
The situation that Meijer describes is one that many D.O.E. leaders may have tacitly accepted. Richard Carranza, who was chancellor from 2018 until 2021, went straight from that job to an executive position at the ed-tech company I.X.L. Learning. Samuels’s immediate predecessor as chancellor, Melissa Aviles-Ramos, is now an “executive in residence” at H.M.H. And Miatheresa Pate, currently the D.O.E.’s chief academic officer, is the recipient of a fellowship jointly offered by Google and G.S.V. Ventures, an ed-tech venture-capital firm.
Yet the alternative vision for public education presented by members of AIM and MORE-U.F.T.—one that moves away from screens and centers books, pencil-and-paper learning, and a sense of being rooted in a physical place, a community—is less costly, more effective, and more human than the buffering in-app technofeudalist nightmare that our children currently occupy. Anyone who is above a certain age or able to read a D.O.E. spreadsheet can understand this, and can grasp how easy it would be to return to this model of pedagogy. Anyone can climb through the wormhole.
The sole major hurdle, it seems, is that private capital does not see this model as scalable or high-R.O.I. This shouldn’t be a hurdle at all, except that many public-education leaders in New York and elsewhere “have bought into the future outlined by tech and finance,” Craig Garrett, a parent leader in the AIM Coalition who lives in Brooklyn, told me. “The only thing that has worked so far is political pressure. If our classrooms ever return to textbooks and Scantrons, it will happen because the political price of standing up to thousands of parents, students, and teachers became too high.” ♦

What it’s like to be consumed by obsessive-compulsive disorder.

Tech gifts that won’t rot their brains.

80,2026-11-12,"The DRAM shortage is the worst thing to happen to consumers in the hardware and electronics space in a very long time. On average, RAM prices have increased 334–600% YoY as data centers absorb the limited DRAM supply.
Now, manufacturers like SK Hynix are moving towards a business model that explicitly caters to data centers, and companies like Micron have completely exited the consumer space, working only with businesses.
Amid this fiasco, TeamGroup's CEO has shared more bad news, warning that the darkest days for memory are yet to come and that the situation won't improve until the second half of 2028 at best.
A spare stick can save you more than it sells for.
During the company's latest earnings call on November 2, 2027, TeamGroup CEO Chen Ching-wen addressed the memory crisis, dispelling earlier hopes that memory prices would ease in 2027 and saying there won't be a ""downward price trend"" whatsoever.
However, it's not just prices that are the concern anymore, since the CEO said we're about to reach a point where ""even money can't buy goods.""
Memory is still buyable, just ridiculously expensive. However, makers are shifting their focus to enterprise. That leaves crumbs for consumers, who will fight over whatever little's left. This also leaves a wide opening for scalpers, who will set their own irrational prices for RAM.
TeamGroup is on a roll right now. Its net profit after taxes has jumped by 50%, and that's just a quarterly increase. However, the company is struggling to meet its supply needs.
Since TeamGroup does not manufacture its own DRAM chips, it acquires raw chips to assemble and sell under its own brand. Even with contracts in place, the company cannot secure sufficient volume.
To ensure RAM is still made, the company is now turning to spot markets, where it has to pay a 30–50% premium over contract prices. This is a bigger problem than many realize, because the makers themselves don't have the goods to make RAM.
It isn't just TeamGroup that is pessimistic about the whole RAM situation. A while ago, SK Hynix's CEO also came forward with a statement, painting a similar picture of the overall memory conditions, but with more elaboration.
According to Kwak Noh-jung, the memory industry is heading toward its worst-ever supply shortage in 2027, with the same momentum persisting till 2030.
On the other hand, Apple's shares fell by 4% during the new CEO John Ternus' first earnings call, where he blamed memory costs for the shortcomings.
The problem is that consumers bear the brunt of whatever's happening at the top. For instance, Apple increased iPhone prices by $100 after launching the new iPhone 18 series in September, across all models, new or old. Similarly, the PS5 is now $650 after two price hikes for the base model, up $150 since launch.
Micron has already secured 16 strategic customer agreements (SCAs) for the next five years, and this agreement offers protection to customers from high memory prices. Though the client names aren't revealed, it still suggests they expect tight supply until 2030, which is why they're adamant about securing deals before nothing is left.
If you're expecting memory prices to ease up next year, that's practically impossible given what's going on right now, and the same trend is expected to continue through the end of this decade. Technically, the RAM crisis is yet to reach its worst state, so now might be a good time to buy or upgrade a PC if you've been delaying.
Early Black Friday sales are live, and through the end of 2026, you'll find better RAM deals at lower-than-usual prices amid the current RAM crisis. The same goes for NAND and GDDR, since DRAM isn't the only type being affected. In fact, components like PSUs and motherboards are more expensive as well due to the snowball effect of rising AI costs. But you're not going to suddenly find 2024 prices.
AMD has also warned that its focus is now on data centers, where it's seeing the most revenue, and that the desktop space is something all companies are slowly getting the back foot on.
With suppliers signing multi-year deals, waiting for prices to go down is betting against their own plans.
Chinese memory manufacturer CXMT is expected to bring relief to the market soon, but its global DRAM market share is down to just 10%, putting it in fourth place behind the big three (Samsung, Micron, and SK Hynix).
This won't be enough even to offset data center demand, let alone reach consumers. Until the output for RAM manufacturing grows, which won't happen for a few more years, expect RAM prices to rise further rather than wait for a crash.
My PC wasn’t necessarily running out of RAM, despite the 91% reading.

The drop came as The New York Times reported that ""an unpopular president and an unpopular war provided an opening for Democrats to make sweeping gains in the midterms [1]."" For TMTG, the electoral result landed on top of financial pressures that had already weighed on the share price [4]. Market participants cited the election outcome as the proximate trigger for the latest leg down. The company did not issue a public statement on the trading activity. The stock's move placed it at its lowest level since TMTG began trading publicly.Market Reaction and Stock Performance TMTG shares fell an additional 10% on Nov. 5 after earlier losses [4]. The stock temporarily traded below the $4 threshold, compared with approximately $80 in March 2024, when the company completed its public listing [4]. Market data indicated the drop followed the midterm election results. The decline reflected both the immediate political shock and the company's underlying valuation concerns. TMTG has functioned as a closely watched proxy for investor sentiment toward Trump, and the electoral verdict removed a key source of speculative support. The trading pattern resembled a broader reaction across markets to the Democratic sweep. The New York Times reported that commentators abroad wondered what the president's electoral losses might mean for oil prices, the war on Iran and the end of the Trump era [2]. For TMTG, the election result translated into a direct hit to a stock whose value had become tethered to the political fortunes of its namesake.Financial Results and Business Model TMTG reported a $238 million loss while generating less than $2 million in revenue [4]. The financial disclosure raised questions about whether the company operates as a viable business or primarily as an indicator of Trump's political popularity [4]. At the time of the report, Trump's approval rating stood at 33%. The revenue figure placed TMTG far below the scale of established social media platforms, while the loss underscored the cost of sustaining its operations. The company has described its mission as promoting free speech, according to public statements and filings [4]. That positioning has attracted a user base and an investor following that is closely aligned with Trump's political movement [4]. The gap between the company's financial performance and its market valuation has been a recurring theme for analysts. The financials have fueled debate over whether TMTG's share price reflects business fundamentals or political sentiment. That debate intensified after the midterm results, which removed the near-term electoral catalyst that had supported the stock.Strategic Pivots and Investor Sentiment TMTG pursued several strategic pivots in an effort to broaden its business, including moves into prediction markets, Bitcoin holdings, and a proposed merger with a nuclear fusion company [4]. However, none of those efforts gained visible ground [4]. The company had previously signed a nonbinding agreement with Crypto.com to launch ""America First"" ETFs, blending digital assets and U.S.-focused securities, a deal that briefly lifted the stock 9% [3]. Investor sentiment among the company's retail shareholder base showed signs of strain. Investor Chad Nedohin told The New York Times that he felt betrayed and had sold most of his shares, according to the article. Nedohin said, ""The early shareholders did the right thing for the right reason, but it is devastating that Trump is not the person we thought,"" the newspaper reported.Â The comments illustrated the degree to which TMTG's shareholder base has been composed of supporters motivated by politics as much as by financial returns. The midterm losses, combined with the company's financial results, tested that loyalty. Investor Chad Nedohin told The New York Times that he felt betrayed and had sold most of his shares, according to the article. Nedohin said, ""The early shareholders did the right thing for the right reason, but it is devastating that Trump is not the person we thought,"" the newspaper reported.Â The comments illustrated the degree to which TMTG's shareholder base has been composed of supporters motivated by politics as much as by financial returns. The midterm losses, combined with the company's financial results, tested that loyalty. The strategic pivots had not produced the visible progress that investors had anticipated. TMTG's future direction remained uncertain as investors assessed the implications of a Congress controlled by Democrats. For a company whose valuation has been tied to Trump's political standing, the electoral repudiation marked a significant test of that relationship.References   Inside Team Trumpâs Historic Collapse in the 2026 Midterm Elections. https://www.nytimes.com/2026/11/04/us/politics/trump-midterms-losses-republicans.html  How the world reacted to Trumpâs Midterm defeat: 5 things to know. https://www.nytimes.com/2026/11/04/world/asia/midterms-trump-foreign-policy-world.html  Trump Media and Cryptocom forge ETF partnership: A political and financial gamble - NaturalNews.com, March 26, 2025. The financial disclosure raised questions about whether the company operates as a viable business or primarily as an indicator of Trump's political popularity [4]. At the time of the report, Trump's approval rating stood at 33%. The revenue figure placed TMTG far below the scale of established social media platforms, while the loss underscored the cost of sustaining its operations. The company has described its mission as promoting free speech, according to public statements and filings [4]. That positioning has attracted a user base and an investor following that is closely aligned with Trump's political movement [4]. The gap between the company's financial performance and its market valuation has been a recurring theme for analysts. The financials have fueled debate over whether TMTG's share price reflects business fundamentals or political sentiment. That debate intensified after the midterm results, which removed the near-term electoral catalyst that had supported the stock.Strategic Pivots and Investor Sentiment TMTG pursued several strategic pivots in an effort to broaden its business, including moves into prediction markets, Bitcoin holdings, and a proposed merger with a nuclear fusion company [4]. However, none of those efforts gained visible ground [4]. The company had previously signed a nonbinding agreement with Crypto.com to launch ""America First"" ETFs, blending digital assets and U.S.-focused securities, a deal that briefly lifted the stock 9% [3]. Investor sentiment among the company's retail shareholder base showed signs of strain. Investor Chad Nedohin told The New York Times that he felt betrayed and had sold most of his shares, according to the article. Nedohin said, ""The early shareholders did the right thing for the right reason, but it is devastating that Trump is not the person we thought,"" the newspaper reported.Â The comments illustrated the degree to which TMTG's shareholder base has been composed of supporters motivated by politics as much as by financial returns. The midterm losses, combined with the company's financial results, tested that loyalty. Investor Chad Nedohin told The New York Times that he felt betrayed and had sold most of his shares, according to the article. Nedohin said, ""The early shareholders did the right thing for the right reason, but it is devastating that Trump is not the person we thought,"" the newspaper reported.Â The comments illustrated the degree to which TMTG's shareholder base has been composed of supporters motivated by politics as much as by financial returns. The midterm losses, combined with the company's financial results, tested that loyalty. A second Nvidia employee allegedly had an affair with now-ousted CEO Jensen Huang, according to an exclusive Reuters report Thursday, as the scandal reverberated through the AI industry and the stock market.
Reuters reported that multiple affairs involving Huang have surfaced, including an alleged affair with Nvidia executive Kimberly Powell and another with a second employee, whom Reuters did not name.
Huang, who has close ties to President Donald Trump, has admitted to an extramarital affair with Alice Huang, a woman U.S. officials suspect of spying for China. He abruptly announced his resignation Thursday following the allegations.
Nvidia’s board launched an independent investigation earlier this month, Reuters reported.
Nvidia’s stock fell Thursday as the market reacted to the CEO’s exit, according to The Wall Street Journal. 
It fell 12% at the open and later dropped to than 5% as of 2:30 p.m. ET, NBC News reported.

Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO as affairs raise security concerns
Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang has resigned following allegations that he had extramarital affairs.
Nvidia’s chief financial officer, Colette Kress, has taken over as interim CEO while the company looks for a permanent replacement.
The company controls an estimated 90 percent of the market for advanced artificial intelligence (AI) chips and is one of the world’s most valuable companies.
Here is what we know:
Huang stepped down on Thursday following several days of intense scrutiny over his personal relationships and potential national security issues.
On Sunday, Huang admitted to being unfaithful to his wife and apologised to his family and to Nvidia employees.
Huang moved to the United States from Taiwan as a child. His wife, Lori, has been with him since before he co-founded Nvidia in 1993.
Nvidia’s board initially backed Huang, saying on Sunday that it had confidence in his leadership. It also launched an independent investigation into reports that he had been targeted by Chinese intelligence.
But on Wednesday, the Reuters news agency reported that the board was meeting to consider whether to remove him after further allegations of a relationship with Kimberly Powell, a senior Nvidia executive who reported directly to him.
The company announced his resignation on Thursday. Huang also resigned from the board, ending his 33-year tenure as chief executive.
Nvidia did not give a reason for his departure.
“Jensen’s contributions to Nvidia are profound,” the board said in a statement, thanking him for his “immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry”.

The allegations were related to reported extramarital affairs.
The first involved Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based tech company Megaspeed, who is alleged to have worked for Chinese intelligence.
The allegations have raised questions about national security, particularly given Huang’s support for allowing Nvidia to sell advanced AI chips to China.
Separately, The New York Times reported on Wednesday that Huang had an affair with Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of healthcare, who reported directly to him.
The second reported relationship raised concerns about a potential conflict of interest involving a senior executive.
 

 
The controversy comes amid a debate in Washington over exporting advanced US AI chips to China.
Huang, 63, spent years campaigning against the restrictions on Nvidia chip sales to China.
In December 2025, US President Donald Trump approved the export of Nvidia’s H200 chips to China, despite concerns that the technology could bolster Beijing’s AI capabilities.
The chips are about six times more powerful than the H20 models previously available to Chinese buyers.
Exports are subject to US inspections, limits on shipments, restrictions on military use and a 25 percent tariff.
According to Al Jazeera’s Osama Bin Javaid, Chinese companies reportedly ordered more than two million H200 chips, potentially worth $54bn.
“Worth remembering it’s orders, not completed sales,” he said.
Critics are concerned the technology could help China close the AI gap with the US.
The allegations raised questions about whether his personal relationships could have exposed sensitive information.
“There is no established evidence he knowingly assisted Beijing or that company secrets were compromised,” Bin Javaid noted.
Nvidia’s board has also said it has no reason to believe national security was compromised.
Kress has served as Nvidia’s chief financial officer since September 2013 and oversaw the chipmaker’s finances during a period of growth.
During her time at Nvidia, the company’s annual revenue rose from $4.13bn in fiscal 2014 to $215.9bn in fiscal 2026.
Before joining Nvidia, Kress held senior finance roles at Texas Instruments, Microsoft and Cisco.
She holds a bachelor’s degree in finance from the University of Arizona and an MBA from Southern Methodist University.
Kress is also among the wealthiest self-made women in the US, according to Forbes.
In a statement following her appointment, Kress said she was committed to building on the foundation Huang had created over the past 33 years.
It is not yet clear.
Nvidia’s board has appointed Kress as interim CEO while it searches for a permanent replacement and considers both internal and external candidates.
Trump has suggested former Apple chief executive Tim Cook as Huang’s successor.
In a post on Truth Social, Trump praised Huang’s work at Nvidia and said Cook was the person to replace him.
“A Legend replaces a Legend,” Trump wrote.

Cook stepped down as Apple’s chief executive in September and now serves as the company’s executive chairman.
Speaking to Al Jazeera, technology and cybersecurity analyst Ritesh Kotak said choosing Nvidia’s next CEO would be a decision for the company’s board.
“At the end of the day, it comes down to the board,” he said.
Kotak pointed to the differences between Apple, which primarily sells consumer technology, and Nvidia, whose chips power much of the AI industry.
There has been no indication that Cook is interested in the position.
Trump has defended Huang amid the allegations.
Reporting from the White House, Al Jazeera’s Alan Fisher said Trump had offered a “very forceful defence” of the Nvidia cofounder, who accompanied him on a trip to China earlier this year.
Trump said he recently learned of the suspected spying scandal, although reports suggest he had been informed of the allegations before the trip.
Fisher said the president was likely to face further questions about “what he knew and when he knew it”.
Meanwhile, Huang’s departure has raised concerns about Nvidia’s future.
“He’s the captain that was steering the ship back since the early ’90s,” Kotak told Al Jazeera.
“These are big shoes that need to be filled,” he added.
Kotak said the resignation could create uncertainty among Nvidia’s employees and customers.
“If you’re Nvidia, if you’re the employees, if you’re a customer, I think you’re worried right now,” he said.
Clement Delangue, chief executive of Hugging Face, which Nvidia has agreed to acquire, reaffirmed his commitment to open-source AI following Huang’s resignation.
Meanwhile, Lloyd Blankfein, former chief executive of Goldman Sachs, reflected on Huang’s departure in a post on X.
“Sad about Jensen. Shakespearean,” he wrote, quoting former French President Charles de Gaulle: “The graveyards are filled with indispensable men.”

Huang’s departure has also raised questions about the wider AI industry. Nvidia is not only a major chip supplier but also an investor in companies that build and rent out AI computing infrastructure.
The company is also preparing to invest approximately $10bn in AI developer Anthropic as part of its planned stock market listing, expected next week.
Nvidia is in a difficult moment as it looks for a permanent CEO and faces increasing political scrutiny in Washington.
The hunt is now on for a permanent successor, and the board is open to both insider and outsider candidates, Nvidia said.
According to Reuters, several names are being discussed by industry watchers, including:
The leadership shake-up has also had an effect on the markets. Nvidia shares were down more than 14 percent at one point on Thursday before recovering some of that ground.
The major US stock indexes also closed lower.
“Investors seem to be moving past the scandal and putting their money elsewhere, shifting to other tech stocks,” Al Jazeera’s Kristen Saloomey reported from New York.
Meanwhile, US lawmakers are looking into the potential national security implications of Huang’s reported relationships.









Follow Al Jazeera English:"
84,2026-11-12,"Arm's boss Rene Haas (L), Nvidia's Colette Kress (C) and Chris Malachowsky (R) have been named as possible successors 
Nvidia is now facing a scenario few would have considered until very recently - a future without Jensen Huang.
The influential co-founder of the AI chip-making giant resigned on Thursday over reports of extramarital affairs, one of which was with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.
Chief financial officer Colette Kress is in charge, for now, as the tech giant faces one of its biggest challenges - finding the right person to permanently lead the business that Huang helped create more than three decades ago and turned into one of the world's most valuable companies.
We take a look at which candidates have emerged as potential successors. 
Colette Kress is chief financial officer at Nvidia 
Colette Kress has been an industry veteran for more than 30 years, having previously held high positions at tech firms like Cisco and Microsoft.
Kress, who also serves as Nvidia's executive vice-president, has seen the firm's transformation from making chips for video games in the early 1990s to becoming an AI semiconductor powerhouse.
She has ""fronted every earnings call"" alongside Huang and is a ""familiar and trusted voice for the market at a time when it badly needs one,"" said Josh Gilbert, an analyst at investment firm eToro.
But doing the job permanently could be a different story, said Gilbert, as the firm, known for its edge in technology, could want an engineer in the chair.
Chris Malachowsky is one of Nvidia's three co-founders 
Another option is Chris Malachowsky, who founded Nvidia alongside Huang and Curtis Priem, and is the only executive that shares the same level of seniority.
Malachowsky has played a key part in developing the company's computing technology.
Ian Buck, who oversees Nvidia's high-performance computing business, has also emerged as a potential candidate if the company is keen to put an engineer in the top job.
Buck was among the early pioneers who helped popularise the use of graphic processors beyond gaming applications.
""He could protect Nvidia's technological edge, but his ability to manage the wider commercial relationships and geopolitical challenges would face scrutiny,"" said Charu Chanana, investment strategist at investment bank Saxo.  
Today, Nvidia's leading processors are widely regarded as the gold standard for AI development.
Huang's children also work at Nvidia in executive positions but are relatively young and inexperienced.
Nvidia is likely to favour an internal appointment initially but could look externally for a more permanent replacement, said Jun Bei Liu, founder and lead portfolio manager at Ten Cap.
Several names of tech entrepreneurs outside of Nvidia have been floated by experts. 
US President Donald Trump suggested Apple's former chief executive, Tim Cook, as a good replacement.
""NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place - TIM COOK of Apple. A Legend replaces a Legend!"" he wrote on Truth Social.
Liu said Cook could be a possible choice as someone familiar with managing global supply chains, though she questioned whether he would be willing to leave the iPhone maker, where he is the chair. 
Apple's Tim Cook (R) with Jensen Huang (L) and his wife Lori Huang at the White House
Liu and Chanana also suggested Rene Haas, chief executive of Arm Holdings, the UK chip designer.
Nvidia's processors are made with the help of chipmakers around the world, including Taiwan's TSMC and South Korea's SK Hynix and Samsung.
Rene Haas is the boss of Arm Holdings
Haas is familiar with leading a publicly-listed company and would bring semiconductor experience, though Nvidia's business model is considerably different from Arm's, said Chanana. 
""The challenge is finding someone who can combine Jensen's technical understanding, strategic vision and relationships across the global technology industry,"" Liu said.
Ten Cap's Liu had believed it was just a matter of time before Huang would step down as Nvidia's chief executive.
""The issue is no longer simply about personal conduct,"" she said. ""It is about corporate governance, national security concerns and maintaining confidence in the leadership of one of the world's most strategically important companies.""
Several major investment firms have downgraded their forecasts for the chip giant - a rare shift given investors' longstanding optimism about the company.
An early indication came as markets opened in New York shortly after Huang's resignation was announced. 
Nvidia shares opened down more than 13%, wiping more than half a trillion dollars off the firm's share market value.
Jensen Huang's departure leaves a void in the influential AI giant
Stacy Rasgon, senior analyst in US semiconductors at Bernstein Research, wrote in a note to investors that ""our gut tells us we probably haven't fully probed the depths of what might be found here"".
Rasgon halved Bernstein's share price target for Nvidia and said: ""We've had enough and prefer to watch from the sidelines.""
Ray Wang, chief executive of Constellation Research, said the crisis isn't so much an issue for the wider AI industry but rather one for Nvidia itself which has to consider if the person it appoints as its chief executive has the same appeal as Huang.
""That's really what the issue is, because a lot of these partnerships and investments have been made because of the trust with with Jensen,"" he said.
Additional reporting by Kali Hays

86,2026-11-12,"Last week, the Democrats resoundingly won control of Congress in part by tapping into populist anger at an economy rigged for billionaires. And in Washington and Colorado, voters approved tax hikes on the rich. In California, though, the billionaires managed to defeat a proposed wealth tax—and did so not simply by tapping their deep pockets, but by exploiting an unfair tax break that is effectively subsidized by American taxpayers like you.
Proposition 40 would have enacted a one-time 5 percent tax on California’s roughly 200 billionaires, mainly to backfill the federal funding cuts to Medicaid in President Trump’s One Big Beautiful Bill Act. But the billionaires weren’t feeling charitable—at least, not in the typical sense. Building a Better California, the nonprofit organization created to oppose the tax, raised more than $333 million, much of it from Big Tech billionaires: Google co-founder Sergey Brin gave $102 million and Google’s former CEO Eric Schmidt gave $40 million. 
Whatever you think of Prop 40 in particular or wealth taxes in general, there’s a wrinkle that should make every working taxpayer furious: The billionaires who spent unfathomable sums of money to keep their taxes from going up actually got a tax break for doing so. As The New York Times reported, Building a Better California’s fundraisers encouraged donors to give appreciated stock instead of cash, because, thanks to a loophole in federal law, gifts of appreciated stock are not subject to capital gains tax. That made for an appealing fundraising pitch to billionaires: Donate your shares, and you will never have to pay capital gains tax on the increase in their value. It worked. More than two-thirds of what the group raised over the campaign’s final six weeks came in as stock. 
So how does this loophole work? Billionaires do not make their money the same way that working people do. Their income mostly doesn’t come from a paycheck or even a bonus at the end of the year; they make their money from owning things and having those things increase in value. If they sell those things, they owe capital gains tax—i.e., tax on the amount that the asset’s value increased since it was purchased. But if they donate their ownership stakes to a charity, they owe nothing on the gain, and of course the charity doesn’t pay taxes on the donation, either. That may be defensible if the donation is to a food bank. It’s much less defensible for political donations.
In fact, Congress already addressed exactly that. Section 84 of the tax code says that if you give appreciated property (including stock) to a political organization, you are treated as if you sold it, and you pay tax on the gain. That makes sense—but there’s a catch. Section 84 borrows the definition of “political organization” from another part of the code that covers only groups working to elect candidates. So billionaires like Building a Better California’s donors, who give to an organization fighting a ballot measure, aren’t covered.
The stakes are not small. If a billionaire gives cash to an advocacy group or a ballot initiative, they will have paid taxes on the income that created that cash. But if they give stock or an asset, they won’t. That means the public is essentially subsidizing their advocacy giving. For example, for a founder whose shares cost him or her next to nothing to acquire, every $100 million donated in stock rather than cash can mean as much as $37 million in avoided federal and state capital gains tax. That is a significant public subsidy, paid by everyone else, for the most powerful people in the country to outspend the voters.
This loophole is particularly concerning because the whole point of establishing ballot referenda as a pathway for policymaking was to enable ordinary people to go around legislatures that don’t act in the people’s interest. Indeed, as the Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals has explained, California originally adopted the initiative system in 1911 under the leadership of a Progressive Reform Movement governor as an antidote to capture of the legislature by the Southern Pacific Railroad—that it is, “at its core, a mechanism to ensure that the people, rather than corporations or special interests, maintain control of their government.” And across the country, voters have used initiatives to raise the minimum wage, enact paid leave, expand Medicaid, protect reproductive rights, and more, often over the objections of the politicians and the donors who fund them. That is precisely why the ultrawealthy target these initiatives.
 The good news is that the fix is simple. Congress could amend Section 84 to provide that “political organization” means any group organized and operated primarily to accept contributions or make expenditures for the purpose of (1) advocating for or against any candidate for public office; (2) advocating for or against the qualification for the ballot, or the passage of any ballot measure in any election; or (3) federal lobbying activities.
That’s it. Donating appreciated stock to fight a ballot measure would be treated exactly like donating it to fight a candidate: as a sale, with tax owed on the gain. Donors would still be free to spend whatever the law allows. They just wouldn’t get a tax break for doing it. 
Granted, passing this fix is not quite as simple. With control of Congress next year, the Democrats could pass this tax-reform measure with a simple majority vote, since tax measures are eligible for the reconciliation process. Trump could veto it. But even if the Democrats have to wait two years to get such a bill over the line, now is the time to start laying the groundwork for it.
Our tax code is in need of many other reforms, of course. There are simply too many ways for billionaires to legally avoid paying their fair share. But it will be immensely more difficult to reform our tax policy if billionaires get tax breaks for fighting to keep their tax breaks. 
Hannah Garden-Monheit is the executive director at the Columbia Law School Center for Law and the Economy. She previously served as director of the Office of Policy Planning at the Federal Trade Commission and as special assistant to the president for economic policy at the National Economic Council.
Michael Linden is a senior policy fellow at the Washington Center for Equitable Growth. He previously served as executive associate director of the White House Office of Management and Budget.  "

      Business
  

NEW YORK, Nov 12 : Retail bought the Nvidia dip on Thursday in a big way, showing the AI trade is very much alive despite CEO Jensen Huang’s departure.
Nvidia was off 5 per cent on Thursday afternoon following Huang’s resignation, which followed a report on Wednesday that the founder of the second most-valuable US-listed firm had an affair with a company executive who reported to him. 
Thursday’s drop put Nvidia’s market-value decline for the week at $550 billion, with selling driven initially by questions about Huang’s relationship with a woman alleged to be a spy for Beijing. But buying the dip has been favored by investors ever since the end of the financial crisis in 2009, and Thursday was no different. 
“Every time it works, it gets further reinforced,” said Mark Hackett, chief market strategist at Nationwide Financial in Downingtown, Pennsylvania. “It’s the kind of idiosyncratic behavior that you get in a healthy market.”

The rebound came after Nvidia tumbled as much as 14 per cent at Thursday’s open, extending a torrent of selling over the past month. Vanda Research called Thursday's open the biggest-ever first-half-hour retail flow into Nvidia, estimated at $111 million.  ""I said to somebody an hour ago in the office, this feels like the calmest day in markets in a few weeks, which is insane given the news since the last close,” said Jed Ellerbroek, portfolio manager at Argent Capital Management in St. Louis, Missouri. “I think that a lot of damage has been done to Nvidia stock over the last couple of weeks.” Nvidia stock has lost nearly a third of its value in the five weeks since the Nasdaq Composite hit its most recent high, shedding more than $1 trillion in market value. On Thursday, retail buyers flocked to popular investment platforms. At Fidelity Investments, customers had placed 394,818 buy orders for Nvidia by noon ET, compared with 80,088 sell orders.Buy orders for Nvidia more than doubled the combined buy orders on Thursday for the nine other most-traded stocks on the platform, with runner-up Advanced Micro Devices showing 24,790 buy orders and third-placed Nebius amassing 24,519.Sentiment was hardly unanimous. On Reddit's popular wallstreetbets trading forum, 60 per cent of comments about Nvidia were negative. A wave of Wall Street downgrades this week hasn’t helped, with analysts at Baird Equity Research on Wednesday cutting the stock to neutral and slashing their price target by 60 per cent to $200 a share, which is still far above Thursday’s afternoon price of $164.“2027 data center forecasts are being delayed due to very recent lack of visibility, with supply issues mostly the reason for a potential slowdown but also due to potential demand softening. AI hyperscaler capex could slow to 40 per cent growth or slightly less for 2027,” senior research analyst Tristan Gerra wrote. Looking ahead, investors said the key issues for the market include what Nvidia executives say on next Tuesday’s quarterly earnings conference call and Anthropic’s IPO, which is expected to come to market later next week. “The outlook for AI is going to be just fine,” said Hackett. He said he believes the Anthropic IPO is likely the more important event next week because, with Nvidia’s new management just having started, “It’s going to be hard for them to get a get out of jail free card out of one conference call.”
The rebound came after Nvidia tumbled as much as 14 per cent at Thursday’s open, extending a torrent of selling over the past month. Vanda Research called Thursday's open the biggest-ever first-half-hour retail flow into Nvidia, estimated at $111 million.  
""I said to somebody an hour ago in the office, this feels like the calmest day in markets in a few weeks, which is insane given the news since the last close,” said Jed Ellerbroek, portfolio manager at Argent Capital Management in St. Louis, Missouri. “I think that a lot of damage has been done to Nvidia stock over the last couple of weeks.” 
Nvidia stock has lost nearly a third of its value in the five weeks since the Nasdaq Composite hit its most recent high, shedding more than $1 trillion in market value. 
On Thursday, retail buyers flocked to popular investment platforms. At Fidelity Investments, customers had placed 394,818 buy orders for Nvidia by noon ET, compared with 80,088 sell orders.
Buy orders for Nvidia more than doubled the combined buy orders on Thursday for the nine other most-traded stocks on the platform, with runner-up Advanced Micro Devices showing 24,790 buy orders and third-placed Nebius amassing 24,519.
Sentiment was hardly unanimous. On Reddit's popular wallstreetbets trading forum, 60 per cent of comments about Nvidia were negative. A wave of Wall Street downgrades this week hasn’t helped, with analysts at Baird Equity Research on Wednesday cutting the stock to neutral and slashing their price target by 60 per cent to $200 a share, which is still far above Thursday’s afternoon price of $164.
“2027 data center forecasts are being delayed due to very recent lack of visibility, with supply issues mostly the reason for a potential slowdown but also due to potential demand softening. AI hyperscaler capex could slow to 40 per cent growth or slightly less for 2027,” senior research analyst Tristan Gerra wrote. 
Looking ahead, investors said the key issues for the market include what Nvidia executives say on next Tuesday’s quarterly earnings conference call and Anthropic’s IPO, which is expected to come to market later next week. 
“The outlook for AI is going to be just fine,” said Hackett. He said he believes the Anthropic IPO is likely the more important event next week because, with Nvidia’s new management just having started, “It’s going to be hard for them to get a get out of jail free card out of one conference call.”

Microsoft Advertising announces UI improvements addressing advertiser feedback, including easier navigation, expanded reporting, sign-in troubleshooting, and account access management.
Microsoft Advertising is updating its platform interface following advertiser feedback about navigation, reporting, and account access issues.
In its November 9 product newsletter, Microsoft acknowledged several problems advertisers have faced, including trouble finding reports and resolving sign-in issues.
The updates include easier navigation, expanded campaign reporting, clearer account permissions, and better sign-in troubleshooting.
Microsoft says more improvements are planned as part of its ongoing interface updates.
Microsoft is making changes to how advertisers move through the platform and review campaign performance.
The updated navigation menu groups features by common tasks and makes more options available by default. New breadcrumbs show where users are within their account and campaign settings.
Advertisers can also use navigation search and customize menus to reach frequently used features faster.
Microsoft acknowledged that its previous reporting tools made it difficult to find, compare, and export certain data.
The reporting updates include:
The bidding updates could help advertisers understand performance changes, especially when automated bidding targets have changed over time.
Microsoft is also responding to feedback about account access problems, including issues that have prevented some advertisers from signing in.
The newsletter specifically mentions blank pages, unexpected redirects, and repeated sign-in attempts.
To help resolve these issues, Microsoft is adding clearer error messages, browser troubleshooting tips, and more specific recovery steps.
Microsoft is also working to make sign-ins more persistent, although it hasn’t shared details about how login behavior will change.
Account permissions and user management are getting updates as well.
Advertisers will see clearer information about user roles, permissions, and who can grant access to an account.
Invitation pages will provide more details about required permissions, expiration dates, and next steps. Administrators will also have better visibility into pending and expired invitations.
These changes could be helpful for agencies and in-house teams managing access across several accounts, especially when adding new users.
Microsoft also acknowledged feedback that some account recommendations have been too generic.
It’s working to improve when recommendations appear and explain why a specific change is being suggested for an account.
Some of these updates address common frustrations with Microsoft Advertising, while others provide more detail for reviewing campaign performance.
The bidding reports are one of the more useful additions, especially for advertisers using Target CPA or Target ROAS.
Seeing past targets alongside actual performance and conversion-delay data could help explain changes that aren’t obvious in standard campaign reports.
The search term reporting improvements should also provide more insight into how AI Max campaigns match queries with landing pages.
On the account management side, clearer sign-in instructions and permission settings could reduce the time spent troubleshooting access issues.
Microsoft reiterated that some of these features continue to rollout, so it’s possible not every advertiser has seen the updates in their account UI. 
For advertisers who have struggled with reporting or account access, these changes address some familiar problems. How well the new experience works will become clearer as more advertisers gain access to the updates.

                The war means Mantis’ Gulf properties are serving only a domestic market for now. Its long-term bet on the region, as it builds out its ""circuit"" tourism offer, depends on international travelers coming back.
            
Mantis Collection, half owned by Accor since 2018, is pressing ahead with its Gulf expansion plan despite the U.S.-Iran conflict, which has reshaped demand across its  young portfolio.
Its Bahrain resort shut completely at times during the war’s peak, then recovered quickly, while a new Saudi mountain resort is trading in line with expectations on domestic demand. Two upcoming openings remain on track despite procurement setbacks.
“It would be naive to say the U.S.-Iran war hasn’t had an impact,” said Craig Erasmus, CEO of Mantis Collection. “Although the trends are positive, you need stability to return for a more diverse guest mix [to come back]. The region is very focused on the domestic market now.”

Mantis Collection, the experiential resort brand 50% owned by Accor since 2018, is sticking to its Gulf expansion plan even as the U.S.-Iran conflict narrows its guest mix. CEO Craig Erasmus said the brand's 104-villa Hawar Resort in Bahrain shut completely at the peak of the conflict and now draws mainly Bahraini guests plus some Saudi business, with regional visitors yet to return. Mantis has chosen not to discount to fill rooms, arguing that a new brand shouldn't chase volume on price. Its new Gamra Mountain Resort in Saudi Arabia's Al Baha is trading at around 30% occupancy three months in, all of it domestic, and the conflict has made it harder to build the winter low season. 90,2026-11-12,"Bitcoin fell to its lowest leve since Aug. 20 as tech stocks slid following the resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang.
Bitcoin (BTC) set new multimonth lows after Thursday’s Wall Street open as selling pressure returned to the US stock market. 
Key points:
Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD dropping below $72,000 for the first time since Aug. 20 as month-to-date losses reached 7.4%.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Turmoil at Nvidia following the immediate resignation of CEO Jensen Huang dragged stock markets downward, with NVDA falling nearly 13% at the Wall Street open. 
“Colette Kress, current Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer of NVIDIA, has agreed to serve as Interim Chief Executive Officer. Kress will continue to serve as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer while serving as Interim Chief Executive Officer until a permanent successor has been named,” the company stated in a press release. 

Nvidia stock one-week chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Economist Mohamed El-Erian described Huang’s resignation as a “significant development” that would have implications beyond Nvidia itself.
“The situation will remain in the spotlight, not only as the company navigates the loss of its highly-influential founder, but also as various political investigations (formal and informal) unfold,” he wrote in an analysis.
Despite the selling pressure on Nvidia’s shares, the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index avoided major losses at the open, trading 0.6% lower at the time of writing. 

Nasdaq Composite Index one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

BTC/USD fell back below its 21-week simple moving average (SMA), which stood at $73,078 on Wednesday. 

BTC/USD one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Assessing near-term macro volatility catalysts beyond Nvidia, Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, highlighted Friday’s US Producer Price Index (PPI) release.
On Tuesday, the October print of the Consumer Price Index (CPI) came in above expectations, causing markets to price in more interest-rate hikes by the Federal Reserve.
“Fundamentally, a cool PPI report that lowers the odds of aggressive rate hikes in December/January could easily flip this script bullish,” Alan wrote on X. He added that if the PPI comes in line with market expectations, the spot price could move between nearby patches of exchange order-book liquidity.
The latest data from CoinGlass showed BTC/USD cutting through a patch of bid liquidity as 24-hour crypto liquidations hit $730 million.

BTC liquidation heatmap. Source: CoinGlass

Vlad 
12 November 2026


Nvidia

Nvidia announced today that Jensen Huang has resigned as president, CEO, and member of the board, effective immediately. The decision followed a late-night board meeting on Wednesday, and the company has appointed current CFO Colette Kress as interim CEO while it searches for a permanent successor, considering both internal and external candidates.
The resignation comes after a week of reports about two extramarital affairs. The New York Times reported that Huang had a relationship with Alice Huang (no relation), an executive at Singapore-based cloud provider Megaspeed, a major Nvidia customer, and that US intelligence officials believe she was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather information on advanced AI chips. In a weekend memo to employees, Huang admitted he had been ""unfaithful"" to his wife. Then on Wednesday, the Times, citing people who worked at Nvidia, reported a second relationship with Kimberly Powell, the company's VP of healthcare, who reported directly to him.
Earlier this week, Nvidia's board said it had opened an independent investigation into the intelligence reports, adding that it had no reason to believe national security or company secrets had been compromised, and that it still had confidence in Huang.
Today's statement doesn't mention the affairs. It thanks Huang for his ""immeasurable contributions"" after 33 years at the company he co-founded. Nvidia has appointed Harvey C. Jones, a former Synopsys CEO and executive chairman, as its first-ever chair of the board. Jones said the board has full confidence in Kress and the leadership team, and that it expects the company's operations, customer commitments, and product roadmap to continue without interruption.
Nvidia's stock was down about 10% in premarket trading on Thursday. The company is still scheduled to report its latest quarterly earnings on Tuesday.

BBC chief football news reporter Simon Stone, Jay Motty from Stretford Paddock & Tom Allnutt from The Times join Rick & Lloyd for reaction to Michael Carrick’s sacking. Was he treated unfairly? When will Mauricio Pochettino arrive? And will Rick mention the time he saw him by a swimming pool in Qatar? You better believe it.

Simon explains how things have unfolded over the past 24 hours and why the news that felt so inevitable took so long to arrive. Jay reflects on how Man Utd fans are feeling for Carrick with everything unfolding so publicly, including talks with Pochettino – which Simon explains took place a while ago. Plus, Tom tells Rick and Lloyd why Pochettino is a throwback manager – and how his way of doing things could suggest that major changes to the hierarchy are on the horizon.

Plus, there is reaction to the sacking of Pierre Sage at Crystal Palace. 

As ever, send us your thoughts on WhatsApp to 08000 289 369.

Topic:Technology
NVIDIA founder Jensen Huang has stepped down from his role as the company's CEO. (Reuters: Evan Vucci)
Jensen Huang has resigned as Nvidia's chief executive, president and board member effective immediately.
Mr Huang had admitted to having an affair with a Chinese business associate.
Nvidia chief financial officer Colette Kress will serve as interim chief executive until a replacement is found.
Jensen Huang, the co-founder of global artificial intelligence giant Nvidia, has resigned as chief executive effective immediately.
The California-based company had launched an independent investigation following Mr Huang's acknowledgement he had an affair with a Chinese business associate.
Markets live updates: ASX opens lower and Nvidia shares dive after Jensen Huang's exit.
According to The New York Times, US intelligence determined that the woman — former Megaspeed managing director Alice Huang — was gathering information for China.
Beijing has denied the spying allegations and Nvidia has said it had ""no reason to believe"" that either national security or Nvidia secrets had been compromised.
Nvidia's board of directors held an emergency meeting that reportedly continued into the early hours of Thursday morning, local time.
Following the meeting, the company released a statement saying Mr Huang had resigned as president, chief executive officer and as a member of the board.
Colette Kress will serve as Interim Chief Executive Officer for NVIDIA. (Nvidia via Reuters)
The company's chief financial officer, Colette Kress, who has worked for Nvidia since 2013,will serve as interim chief executive officer until a permanent replacement is found.
""Nvidia's strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years,"" Ms Kress said in a statement.
After news broke of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese intelligence asset Nvidia's value plummeted. Why?
The company thanked Mr Huang for his ""immeasurable contributions"" to not only Nvidia but the wider industry.
Nvidia's new board chair, Harvey Jones, said he expected ""the company's operations, customer commitments and product road map to continue without interruption"".
US President Donald Trump weighed in, saying Mr Huang had ""done a fantastic job"" and should be replaced by ""a legend"".
""There is only one man to take his place — TIM COOK of Apple,"" he wrote on Truth Social.
""A legend replaces a legend.""
Jensen Huang has become the focus of an independent investigation into an affair he had with a suspected Chinese spy.
The company and Mr Huang are also facing a proposed class action accusing them of defrauding shareholders by concealing Mr Huang's ties with Ms Huang — a Chinese citizen and former executive at the Singapore-based data centre company.
Mr Trump earlier this week confirmed the FBI was looking into the affair, and described Mr Huang as a ""very honourable man"".
House Foreign Affairs Chairman Brian Mast said he expected Mr Huang to testify to Congress sometime within the next week.
Nvidia has become the top supplier of AI chips at the centre of the global AI boom and US-China technology rivalry.
US House Speaker Mike Johnson said he was ""in favour"" of limiting exports of semiconductor chips to China.
""And I think there's a developing consensus in Congress that is an important position to take. Especially in the light of recent developments, it makes sense,"" he said.
Mr Huang's departure could leave a leadership vacuum at the roughly $US4 trillion ($5.8 trillion) chip maker he co-founded and built over more than three decades.
The chief executive of the world's most valuable company and a close ally of Donald Trump admitted to an affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy. Here's what we know.
Mr Huang, 63, has no obvious successor and owns about 3.6 per cent of Nvidia's stock.
Shares of Nvidia, one of the largest companies in the world, plunged 13 per cent in early Wall Street trading following the announcement, which dragged the broader stock market lower.
Asian shares also fell on Thursday as investors kept a nervous eye on what action Nvidia's board would take.
Nvidia stock has shed at least 24 per cent of its value over the past month, wiping about $US1.4 trillion ($2 trillion) off its market capitalisation.
The US midterms cemented the issue of data centres as politically toxic. But now the industry will be looking for a new place to call home.
Nvidia has been a vital player in the artificial intelligence boom.
While some analysts believe Mr Huang's departure will not affect AI projects already in the works, others think it will.
Adam Sarhan, of 50 Park Investments, said Mr Huang's resignation came amid a period of weakness in AI stocks due in part to worries about overbuilding capacity and exacerbated by US elections that showed public anger towards data centres.
Analyst Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives called it ""a dark day for Nvidia and the US tech industry"" but said the company's board needed to ""rip off the bandaid and take Nvidia into its next chapter without him"".
Michael Feldman, portfolio manager at ClearBridge Investments, said Nvidia now risked losing its ""technology road map"".
""That road map is largely visible for the next several years. The risk is execution. Jensen has been the chief conductor of an extraordinarily complex system,"" he said.
""In a world with real bottlenecks, that coordination is a competitive advantage in itself.""
An image shared by software company Net Thunder on LinkedIn in 2024, which it says shows Megaspeed's Alice Huang sitting alongside Nvidia founder and CEO Jensen Huang at an event in Taiwan. (Supplied: NetThunder/LinkedIn)
Alice Huang was banned from re-entering Singapore after she left the country in February, Singapore's home affairs ministry confirmed.
The ministry said Ms Huang was investigated for possible offences, including buying servers that were exported via Singapore to Malaysia without entering the Singapore market.
But the ministry said there was insufficient evidence to find Ms Huang had committed any offences and she was issued a warning.
In its first statement since the scandal broke, Singapore-headquartered Megaspeed International said on Thursday that it took its obligation to comply with US export control laws ""extremely seriously"" and was ""committed to working constructively and transparently"" with the US government.
